The last date for submission of public comments is indicated on each Notice of Proposed Rule Making. Unless a
different date is speciﬁed by statute, the proposing agency must accept comments for at least: 60 days after the date of
Register publication of a Notice of Proposed Rule Making or combined Notice of Emergency Adoption and Proposed
Rule Making; and 45 days after publication of a Notice of Revised Rule Making or combined Notice of Emergency
Adoption and Revised Rule Making. When a public hearing on a proposed rule is statutorily required: the hearing
may not be held until at least 60 days after the publication date of the notice; and comments must be accepted for at
least 5 days after the last required hearing. When a public comment period for a proposed rule is scheduled to end on
a Saturday, Sunday or public holiday, comments are accepted through the next succeeding business day.
For notices published in this issue:
– the 60-day period expires on November 29, 2026
– the 45-day period expires on November 14, 2026

Be a part of the rule making process!
Public comment on proposed rules is encouraged and may be submitted to the agency that is proposing
the rule. Address your comments to the agency representativ e whose name and address is printed in the rule
making notice. No special form is required; a handwritten le tter will do. Individuals who access the online
Register (dos.ny.gov/state-register) may send public comment via electronic mail to e-mail addresses that may
be provided in Notices of Proposed Rule Making. This include s Proposed, Emergency/Proposed, Revised
Proposed and Emergency/Revised Proposed rule makings.
To be considered, comments should reach the agency before expiration of the public comment period.
The State Administrative Procedure Act provides for a minimum 60-day public comment period after publica-
tion in the Register for Notices of Proposed Rule Making, and a 45-day public comment period for Notices of
Revised Rule Making. If a public hearing is required by statute, public comments are accepted for at least ﬁve
days after the last such hearing. Agencies are also required to specify in each notice the last date of the public
comment period.
When a public comment period would end on a Saturday or Sunday , the agency accepts public com-
ment through the following Monday; when the comment period ends on a public holiday, public comment will
be accepted through the next succeeding business day. Agencies cannot adopt a proposed rule until the day af-
ter the conclusion of the public comment period.
The Administrative Regulations Review Commission (ARRC) reviews newly proposed regulations to
examine issues of compliance with legislative intent, impact on the economy, and impact on affected parties.
In addition to sending comments or recommendations to the ag ency, please do not hesitate to transmit your
views to ARRC:
Administrative Regulations Review Commission
State Capitol
Albany, NY 12247
Telephone: (518) 455-2731

RULE MAKING
ACTIVITIES
Each rule making is identiﬁed by an I.D. No., which consists
of 13 characters. For example, the I.D. No. AAM-01-96-
00001-E indicates the following:
AAM -the abbreviation to identify the adopting agency
01 -the State Register issue number
96 -the year
00001 -the Department of State number, assigned upon
receipt of notice.
E -Emergency Rule Making—permanent action
not intended (This character could also be: A
for Adoption; P for Proposed Rule Making; RP
for Revised Rule Making; EP for a combined
Emergency and Proposed Rule Making; EA for
an Emergency Rule Making that is permanent
and does not expire 90 days after ﬁling.)
Italics contained in text denote new material. Brackets
indicate material to be deleted.
Ofﬁce of Children and Family
Services
NOTICE OF ADOPTION
Diligence of Efforts Requirements and Permanency Planning for
Y outh in Foster Care
I.D. No. CFS-46-25-00014-A
Filing No. 860
Filing Date: 2026-09-09
Effective Date: 2026-09-30
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following action:
Action taken: Amendment of section 430.12 of Title 18 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Social Services Law, sections 20(3)(d), 34(3)(f) and
398(6)
Subject: Diligence of efforts requirements and permanency planning for
youth in foster care.
Purpose: To provide greater clarity and speciﬁcity for permanency pl an-
ning requirements for youth in foster care.
Text or summary was published in the November 19, 2025 issue of the
Register, I.D. No. CFS-46-25-00014-P .
Final rule as compared with last published rule: No changes.
Text of rule and any required statements and analyses may be obtained
from: Nicholas Martin, Ofﬁce of Children and Family Services, 52
Washington Street, Rensselaer, New Y ork 12144, (518) 474-8 310, email:
regcomments@ocfs.ny.gov
Initial Review of Rule
As a rule that does not require a RFA, RAFA or JIS, this rule wil l be
initially reviewed in the calendar year 2031, which is no later than the 5th
year after the year in which this rule is being adopted
Assessment of Public Comment
This rule signiﬁcantly reorganizes and amends the regulato ry provi-
sions to provide clarity and improved standards for the dili gent efforts
required for permanency planning for youth in foster care.
The New York State Ofﬁce of Children and Family Services (OCF S)
received one (1) public comment in response to the proposed amendments
during the public comment period. The comment was reviewed a nd
considered by OCFS. No changes were made to the proposed regulations
in response to this comment.
The commenter identiﬁed three (3) key recommendations for c hanges
to the proposed regulations including seeking that OCFS:
(1) clarify deﬁnitions and terminology related to APPLA to e liminate
confusion and ensure consistent application;
(2) strengthen discharge planning and discharge standards by requiring
identiﬁcation of a speciﬁc, stable residence prior to discharge and prohibit-
ing discharge to all shelters or congregate settings; and
(3) preserve and strengthen independent living stipends by maintaining
set rates, providing Cost of Living Adjustments (COLAs), ex tending
eligibility to all youth receiving independent living skills instruction, and
establishing clear, fair rules for suspension.
The Commenter additionally made recommendations that focu s on
expanding and clarifying visitation requirements (including virtual visita-
tion) and providing greater transparency and notice for you th in the pre-
and post- discharge periods. Additionally, regarding the ask that the regula-
tions include an explicit prohibition on discharge when a yo uth is not
meeting educational or vocational requirements, such a requirement is al-
ready set forth in clear terms at 430.12(f)(3)(i)(e), which provides “Y outh
may not be discharged to APPLA solely on the basis of not meeti ng the
educational or vocational standards described in clauses (a) and (b) of this
subparagraph…”
Regarding the ﬁrst key recommendation, OCFS agrees that the re is a
need for clariﬁcation of deﬁnitions and terminology relate d to APPLA.
Such clariﬁcations are not appropriate for inclusion in this regulatory pack-
age but will be developed as part of policy issuance.
Regarding the second key recommendation, OCFS agrees that s table
housing is important and has addressed this critical issue in this regulatory
package. The regulations expressly state at 430.12(f)(3)( i)(e), “Under no
circumstances may a youth be discharged without a plan for st able hous-
ing as identiﬁed in paragraph (4) of this subdivision.” Paragraph (4) goes
on to state that discharge may only occur when there is a reaso nable
expectation that housing will remain available for 12 months unless one of
the exceptions apply. The commenter speciﬁcally takes issu e with the
inclusion of Transitional Independent Living Programs (TI LPs) as an
exception to the twelve-month minimum requirement. Unlike foster care,
TILPs may serve youth up to age 24, for up to 24 months. Some you th
may require additional services and supports to maintain st able housing
for a period of time after they turn 21 and are required to leave foster care.
Including TILPs is necessary to allow the youth ﬂexibility and to provide
developmentally appropriate supports as they transition from foster care.
Regarding the third key recommendation, OCFS will clarify e xpecta-
tions in policy around notice for youth in the pre-and post- d ischarge
periods, including duration of a relationship with a perman ency resource
prior to discharge, notice to youth who have voluntarily lef t their foster
care placement and have been absent for 60 days prior to their anticipated
discharge date, supervision to 21 in accordance with 430.12(f)(5)(ii), dis-
charge from care at age 21, and the youth’s ability to provide a copy of
their Transition Plan to their attorney. Additionally, reg arding the com-
ment around vital documents, this requirement is fully addr essed in both
the updated Transition Plan and the policy guidance release d with that
update, 24-OCFS-ADM-01-R1 titled “Revised New Y ork State Transition
Plan Form to Help Y outh Plan for a Successful Discharge,” as w ell as in
regulation at 430.12(l).
Regarding the additional recommendations around visitati on require-
ments, the ability to use virtual visitation, when appropri ate, is already
permitted. Additionally, to the extent that this comment seeks guidance on
1

virtual casework contacts with the youth in foster care, a regulatory pack-
age is in progress to address the ability to conduct casework contacts virtu-
ally in speciﬁc circumstances, as permitted by the Federal G overnment.
Finally, regarding commenter’s recommendations around deﬁning “impos-
sibility” in this section with regard to biweekly visitatio n requirements,
OCFS acknowledges the importance of continuity in a child’s environ-
ment and maintaining family connections. Of note, regulati ons at 430.11
require documentation in the case record as to the possibili ty or impos-
sibility of biweekly visits and the issues commenter highlights around lack
of due diligence have not borne out in practice observed duri ng OCFS’s
oversight and monitoring. However, given the importance of maintaining
family connections, OCFS will continue to conduct oversigh t and moni-
toring in this regard and counsel for the child(ren) are encouraged to raise
lack of due diligence to the Courts as well.
Regarding the additional recommendations around stipends, please note
that stipends are still included in the regulation at 430.12(f)(3)(i)(f), which
provides, in relevant part, “Districts must ensure that a monthly stipend is
provided to each youth aged 16 or older who has or is deemed to have the
goal of APPLA.” The change to this section was the removal of t he
outdated schedule of stipends which ﬁxed the stipend amount at $20 to
$40 per month, depending on the youth’s age. It was replaced by language
requiring that a stipend be paid and stipend information be c onﬁrmed in
the Family Assessment Service Plan (FASP) or require that the FASP state
the reasons that the stipend is suspended. This change is to allow for ﬂex-
ibility at the local level to consider the individual circum stances of the
child and provide stipends appropriate to their needs. OCFS acknowledges
the need for additional guidance for the ﬁeld on this topic and will provide
such through policy or other agency issuance.
Regarding the additional recommendations around complian ce with
Safe Landings legislation, OCFS published an Informationa l Letter, 25-
OCFS-INF-10, titled “Amendment to the Safe Landings Act for Youth
Leaving Foster Care” on September 23, 2025. That INF informed LDSSs
and V As of the enactment of the Safe Landings Act and the changes to rel-
evant law.

Jurisdictional Classiﬁcation
I.D. No. CVS-39-26-00002-P
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following proposed rule:
Proposed Action: Amendment of Appendices 1 and 2 of Title 4 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Civil Service Law, section 6(1)
Subject: Jurisdictional Classiﬁcation.
Purpose: To classify positions in the exempt and non-competitive classes.
Text of proposed rule: Amend Appendix 1 of the Rules for the Classiﬁed
Service, listing positions in the exempt class, in the Executive Department
under the subheading “Division of Homeland Security and Eme rgency
Services,” by increasing the number of positions of Assista nt Director
Public Information from 1 to 2 and Special Assistant from 25 to 26; and
Amend Appendix 2 of the Rules for the Classiﬁed Service, listing posi-
tions in the non-competitive class, in the Executive Department under the
subheading “Division of Homeland Security and Emergency Services,” by
adding thereto the positions of Interoperable Communications Specialist 1
(DHSES).

PROPOSED RULE MAKING
NO HEARING(S) SCHEDULED
Neighborhood Preservation Crime Prevention Act Guidelines
I.D. No. CJS-39-26-00001-P
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following proposed rule:
Proposed Action: This is a consensus rule making to repeal Part 6160 of
Title 9 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: EXPRESS NY Initiative (Expediting Processes and
Regulations to Enable Streamlined Services) - New York Stat e effort to
identity and reform or eliminate outdated and excessive regulations; Exec-
utive Law, section 837(14) - Division of Criminal Justice Services (DCJS)
authority to “[d]o all other things necessary or convenient to carry out the
functions, powers and duties [of DCJS]”
Subject: Neighborhood Preservation Crime Prevention Act Guidelines.
Purpose: Repeal unnecessary regulations relating to funding progra ms
under the 1983 Neighborhood Preservation Crime Prevention Act.
Text of proposed rule: Repeal of Part 6160 of Title 9 of the New York
Codes, Rules and Regulations.
Text of proposed rule and any required statements and analyses may be
obtained from: Natasha Harvin, Esq. , Division of Criminal Justice Ser-
vices, 80 South Swan Street, Albany, New Y ork 12210, (518) 45 7-8413,
email: dcjslegalrulemaking@dcjs.ny.gov
Data, views or arguments may be submitted to: Same as above.
Public comment will be received until: 60 days after publication of this
notice.
Consensus Rule Making Determination
This proposal merely repeals outdated and excessive regulations as set
forth in Part 6160 of Title 9 NYCRR. The regulations relate to guidelines
for funding crime prevention programs under the 1983 Neighb orhood
Preservation Crime Prevention Act (Executive Law §§ 846-A t hrough
846-G). There is no current funding appropriated for use under the forego-
ing statutes, nor has there been since at least 2009. The Division of Crimi-
nal Justice Services (DCJS) continues to support neighborhoods and crime
prevention efforts through other appropriations. Speciﬁc programs include
Gun Involved Violence Elimination (GIVE), SNUG (guns spell ed
backwards), and Respond, Invest, Sustain, Empower (RISE), whose fund-
ing allocations are based on clearly articulated factors, i ncluding crime
statistics, community input, and other evidence-based factors.
Given the ministerial nature and purpose of the proposal, DC JS has
determined that no person is likely to object to the repeal of the rule.
Job Impact Statement
This proposal merely repeals outdated and excessive regulations as set
forth in Part 6160 of Title 9 NYCRR. The regulations relate to guidelines
for funding crime prevention programs under the 1983 Neighb orhood
Preservation Crime Prevention Act (Executive Law §§ 846-A t hrough
846-G). There is no current funding appropriated for use under the forego-
ing statutes, nor has there been since at least 2009. The Division of Crimi-
nal Justice Services continues to support neighborhoods and crime preven-
tion efforts through other appropriations. Speciﬁc progra ms include Gun
Involved Violence Elimination (GIVE), SNUG (guns spelled backwards),
and Respond, Invest, Sustain, Empower (RISE), whose fundin g alloca-
tions are based on clearly articulated factors, including c rime statistics,
community input, and other evidence-based factors.
As such, it is apparent from the nature and purpose of the proposal that
it will have no impact on jobs and employment opportunities.

RULE MAKING
Supplementary Certiﬁcate and Supplementary Bilingual
Education Extension Requirements
I.D. No. EDU-26-26-00007-E
Filing No. 861
Filing Date: 2026-09-14
Effective Date: 2026-09-14
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following action:
Action taken: Amendment of sections 80-2.9, 80-4.3 and 80-5.18 of Title
8 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Education Law, sections 101, 207, 208, 305, 308,
3001, 3004 and 3009
Finding of necessity for emergency rule: Preservation of general welfare.
Speciﬁc reasons underlying the ﬁnding of necessity: The Board of
Regents permanently adopted regulatory amendments at its January 2024,
November 2024, and October 2025 meetings to the Supplementary certiﬁ-
cate requirements in order to provide ﬂexibility for certiﬁed teachers who
apply for the Supplementary English to Speakers of Other Lan guages
(ESOL) certiﬁcate, and for teachers who hold a valid Initial, Professional,
or Permanent ESOL certiﬁcate and apply for a Supplementary c ertiﬁcate
in another area through August 31, 2026. This ﬂexibility allows candidates
to complete either the examination requirement or the enrol lment and
education requirements. At these same meetings, the Board o f Regents
also permanently adopted regulatory amendments to the Supp lementary
Bilingual Education extension requirements in order to cre ate ﬂexibility
for certiﬁed teachers and certiﬁed pupil personnel services providers who
apply for such extension through August 31, 2026.
The Department now proposes to extend these ﬂexibilities fo r one ad-
ditional year, through August 31, 2027, in order to ensure th at educators
can continue to expeditiously obtain this certiﬁcate and ex tension to ad-
dress the inﬂux of ELLs in schools.
The proposed amendment was presented to the Higher Education Com-
mittee for recommendation to the Full Board for adoption as a n emer-
gency rule at the June 2026 meeting of the Board of Regents, ef fective
June 16, 2026. Since the Board of Regents meets at ﬁxed interv als, the
earliest the proposed amendment could be adopted by regular (nonemer-
gency) action after expiration of the 60-day public comment period
provided for in the State Administrative Procedure Act (SAP A) sections
201(1) and (5) would be the October 2026 Regents meeting. Furthermore,
pursuant to SAPA 203(1), the earlier effective date of the proposed rule, if
adopted at the October 2026 meeting, would be October 21, 2026, the date
the Notice of Adoption would be published in the State Register.

Washington Avenue, Albany, NY 12234, (518) 474-6400, email :

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY:
Education Law § 101 (not subdivided) charges the Department with the
general management and supervision of all public schools an d all of the
educational work of the state.
Education Law § 207 grants general rule-making authority to the
Regents to carry into effect State educational laws and policies.
Education Law § 208 grants general rule-making authority to the
Regents to confer suitable certiﬁcates, diplomas and degre es on persons
who satisfactorily meet the requirements prescribed.
Education Law § 305 authorizes the Commissioner to enforce t he
educational policies of this State and execute all educatio nal policies
determined by the Regents and shall prescribe the licensing of teachers
employed in this State.
Education Law § 308 authorizes the Commissioner to enforce any rule
or direction of the Regents pertaining to the school system o r any part
thereof.
Education Law § 3001 establishes the qualiﬁcations of teach ers in the
classroom.
Education Law § 3004 authorizes the Commissioner to promulg ate
regulations governing the certiﬁcation requirements for teachers employed
in public schools.
Education Law § 3009 prohibits school districts from paying the salary
of an unqualiﬁed teacher.
2. LEGISLA TIVE OBJECTIVES:
The proposed amendment is consistent with the above statutory author-
ity and is necessary to extend the ﬂexibility for the Supplementary Certiﬁ-
cate and the Supplementary Bilingual Education extension r equirements
to continue to address stafﬁng challenges due to the inﬂux of recently ar-
rived and asylum-seeking students.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
In response to the potential shortage of educators needed to support the
inﬂux of ELLs in schools, the Board of Regents adopted amendm ents to
the Supplementary certiﬁcate requirements to provide ﬂexibility for certi-
ﬁed teachers who apply for the Supplementary English to Spea kers of
Other Languages (ESOL) certiﬁcate, and for teachers who hol d a valid
Initial, Professional, or Permanent ESOL certiﬁcate and ap ply for a Sup-
plementary certiﬁcate in another area through August 31, 2026. This ﬂex-
ibility allows candidates to complete either the examinati on requirement
or the enrollment and education requirements. The Departme nt proposes
to extend this ﬂexibility through August 31, 2027.
The Board of Regents also amended the Supplementary Bilingu al
Education extension requirements to create ﬂexibility for certiﬁed teach-
ers and certiﬁed pupil personnel services professionals who apply for such
extension through August 31, 2026. This ﬂexibility allows c andidates to
pass the Bilingual Education Assessment (BEA), if availabl e, in lieu of
matriculating in a registered program leading to a Bilingua l Education
extension and completing coursework. The Department similarly proposes
to extend this ﬂexibility through August 31, 2027. Additionally, the amend-
ment permits a single renewal of the Supplementary Bilingua l Education
extension if a candidate obtains their ﬁrst extension through this ﬂexibility.
This is designed to accommodate candidates who may decide to matricu-
late in a registered Bilingual Education extension program at a later date.
By allowing candidates to demonstrate that they have the kno wledge
and skills to provide the necessary supports to recently arrived and asylum-
seeking students by passing a test instead of completing coursework, this
ﬂexibility provides a quicker pathway to obtaining the Supplementary cer-
tiﬁcate and/or Supplementary Bilingual Education extensi on. This
pathway is also less costly than completing coursework, whi ch can ease
the ﬁnancial barrier for candidates.
4. COSTS:
a. Costs to State government: The proposed amendment does no t
impose any costs on State government.
b. Costs to local government: The proposed amendment does no t
impose any costs on local government.
c. Costs to private regulated parties: The proposed amendment does not
impose any costs on private regulated parties.
d. Costs to regulating agency: The proposed amendment does n ot
impose any costs on the State Education Department for imple mentation
and continued administration.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES:
The proposed amendment merely extends the ﬂexibility provi ded for
the requirements to obtaining the Supplementary Certiﬁcate and the Sup-
plementary Bilingual Education extension to continue to ad dress the
increase in enrollment of students who are speakers of langu ages other
than English. Therefore, the proposed amendment does not im pose any
additional program, service, duty or responsibility upon a ny local
government.
6. PAPERWORK:
The proposed amendment does not impose any additional paper work
requirements.
7. DUPLICA TION:
The proposed amendment does not duplicate existing State or Federal
requirements.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
The proposed amendment is necessary to extend the ﬂexibility provided
for the requirements to obtain the Supplementary Certiﬁcate and the Sup-
plementary Bilingual Education extension to continue to ad dress the
increase in enrollment of students who are speakers of langu ages other
than English. No signiﬁcant alternatives were considered.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS:
Since there are no applicable federal standards, the proposed rule does
not exceed any minimum federal standards for the same or similar subject
areas.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
It is anticipated that the proposed amendment will be presented for per-
manent adoption at the October 2026 Regents meeting, after p ublication
of the proposed amendment in the State Register and expiration of the 60-
day public comment period required under the State Administrative Proce-
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
10

dure Act. Because the emergency action will expire before th e October
2026 Regents meeting, it is anticipated that an additional e mergency ac-
tion will be presented for adoption at the July 2026 meeting. If adopted at
the October 2026 meeting, the proposed amendment will become effective
as a permanent rule on October 21, 2026.
Regulatory Flexibility Analysis
The purpose of the proposed rule is to extend the ﬂexibility p rovided
for the requirements to obtain the Supplementary Certiﬁcate and the Sup-
plementary Bilingual Education extension to continue to ad dress the
increase in enrollment of students who are speakers of langu ages other
than English.
The proposed rule will not have any adverse economic impact, nor
impose any reporting, recordkeeping or other compliance requirements on
small businesses or local governments. Because it is eviden t from the
nature of the proposed amendment that it does not adversely a ffect small
businesses or local governments, no further measures were n eeded to
ascertain that fact and none were taken. Accordingly, a regu latory no
regulatory ﬂexibility analysis is not required pursuant to section 202-
b(3)(a) of the State Administrative Procedure Act, and one h as not been
prepared.
Rural Area Flexibility Analysis
The purpose of the proposed rule is to extend the ﬂexibility p rovided
for the requirements to obtain the Supplementary Certiﬁcate and the Sup-
plementary Bilingual Education extension to continue to ad dress the
increase in enrollment of students who are speakers of langu ages other
than English.
The proposed rule will not have any adverse impact, nor impos ed any
reporting, recordkeeping or other compliance requirement s on public or
private entities in rural areas. Because it is evident from the nature of the
proposed amendment that it does not adversely impact entiti es located in
rural areas, no further measures were needed to ascertain th at fact and
none were taken. Accordingly, a rural area ﬂexibility analy sis is not
required pursuant to section 202-bb(4)(a) of the State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, and one has not been prepared.
Job Impact Statement
The purpose of the proposed rule is to extend the ﬂexibility p rovided
for the requirements to obtain the Supplementary Certiﬁcate and the Sup-
plementary Bilingual Education extension to continue to ad dress the
increase in enrollment of students who are speakers of langu ages other
than English.
Because it is evident from the nature of the proposed amendment that it
will have no impact on jobs or employment opportunities attr ibutable to
its adoption or only a positive impact, no afﬁrmative steps were needed to
ascertain these facts and none were taken. Accordingly, a job impact state-
ment is not required pursuant to section 201-a(2)(a) of the State Adminis-
trative Procedure Act, and one has not been prepared.

PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following action:
Proposed Action: Amendment of section 80-5.3 of Title 8 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Education Law, sections 101, 207, 208, 305, 308,
3001, 3004 and 3009
Finding of necessity for emergency rule: Preservation of general welfare.
Speciﬁc reasons underlying the ﬁnding of necessity: At its December
2020 meeting, the Board of Regents permanently adopted regu latory
amendments to increase the amount of incidental teaching pe rmitted in
schools from up to ﬁve classroom hours a week to up to 10 classr oom
hours a week during the 2020-2021 school year in response to t he
COVID-19 pandemic. Through incidental teaching, certiﬁed teachers can
teach a subject not covered by their certiﬁcate when no certiﬁed or quali-
ﬁed teachers are available after extensive and documented recruitment.
The Board of Regents permanently adopted regulatory amendm ents at
its October 2021, September 2022, September 2023, November 2024, and
November 2025 meetings to extend the ﬂexibility for inciden tal teaching
from the 2021-2022 through the 2025-2026 school years, respectively, to
give districts another way to address stafﬁng challenges. The Department
now proposes to extend the ﬂexibility for incidental teaching again through
the 2026-2027 school year. This proposal enables school dis tricts to ad-
dress their continuing stafﬁng challenges by providing the m with ﬂex-
ibility in making teaching assignments.
Since the Board of Regents meets at ﬁxed intervals, the earli est the
proposed amendment could be adopted by regular (non-emergency) action
after expiration of the 60-day public comment period provid ed for in the
State Administrative Procedure Act (SAPA) sections 201(1) and (5) would
be the January 2027 Regents meeting. Furthermore, pursuant to SAPA
203(1), the earliest effective date of the proposed rule, if adopted at the
January meeting, would be January 27, 2027, the date the Notice of Adop-
tion would be published in the State Register. Therefore, em ergency ac-
tion is necessary at the September 2026 meeting for the preservation of the
general welfare to continue allowing certiﬁed teachers to t each up to 10
classroom hours a week in a subject area not covered by their c ertiﬁcate
for the 2026-2027 school year to address teacher shortages. It is anticipated
that the proposed rule will be presented to the Board of Regents for adop-
tion as a permanent rule at its January 2027 meeting, which is the ﬁrst
scheduled meeting after expiration of the 60-day public com ment period
mandated by SAPA for State agency rulemaking. However, since the emer-
gency action will expire before the January 2027 meeting, it is anticipated
that an additional emergency action will be presented for ad option at the
November 2026 Regents meeting.
Subject: Extending Flexibility for Incidental Teaching.
Purpose: Enable school districts to address ongoing stafﬁng challenges by
providing them with ﬂexibility in making teaching assignments.
Text of emergency/proposed rule: The opening paragraph of section 80-
5.3 of the Regulations of the Commissioner of Education shall be amended
to read as follows:
A superintendent of schools may assign a teacher to teach a subject not
covered by such a teacher’s certiﬁcate or license for a period not to exceed
5 classroom hours a week, and during the 2020-2021 through th e [2025-
2026] 2026-2027 school years for a period not to exceed 10 classroom
hours a week, when no certiﬁed or qualiﬁed teacher is availab le after
extensive and documented recruitment, and provided that approval of the
commissioner is obtained in accordance with the following requirements:
This notice is intended: to serve as both a notice of emergency adoption
and a notice of proposed rule making. The emergency rule will e xpire
December 13, 2026.

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY:
Education Law 101 (not subdivided) charges the Department w ith the
general management and supervision of all public schools an d all of the
educational work of the state.
Education Law 207 grants general rule-making authority to the Regents
to carry into effect State educational laws and policies.
Education Law 208 grants general rule-making authority to the Regents
to confer suitable certiﬁcates, diplomas and degrees on per sons who
satisfactorily meet the requirements prescribed.
Education Law 305 authorizes the Commissioner to enforce th e
educational policies of this State and execute all educatio nal policies
determined by the Regents and shall prescribe the licensing of teachers
employed in this State.
Education Law 308 authorizes the Commissioner to enforce any rule or
direction of the Regents pertaining to the school system or any part thereof.
Education Law 3001 establishes the qualiﬁcations of teache rs in the
classroom.
Education Law 3004 authorizes the Commissioner to promulga te
regulations governing the certiﬁcation requirements for teachers employed
in public schools.
Education Law 3009 prohibits school districts from paying the salary of
an unqualiﬁed teacher.
2. LEGISLA TIVE OBJECTIVES:
NYS Register/September 30, 2026 Rule Making Activities
11

The proposed amendment is consistent with the above statutory author-
ity and is necessary to extend the ﬂexibility for incidental teaching through
the 2026-2027 school year that enables school districts to address ongoing
stafﬁng challenges by providing them with ﬂexibility in making teaching
assignments.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
At its December 2020, October 2021, September 2022, Septemb er
2023, November 2024, and November 2025 meetings, the Board o f
Regents permanently adopted regulatory amendments to incr ease the
amount of incidental teaching permitted in schools from up t o ﬁve
classroom hours a week to up to 10 classroom hours a week durin g the
2020-2021, 2021-2022, 2022-2023, 2023-2024, 2024-2025, a nd 2025-26
school years, respectively. This amendment helped school d istricts ad-
dress stafﬁng challenges related to and exacerbated by the C OVID-19
pandemic. Through incidental teaching, certiﬁed teachers can teach a
subject not covered by their certiﬁcate when the school district determines
that no certiﬁed or qualiﬁed teachers are available. The sch ool district
must engage in extensive and documented recruitment effort s prior to
making such a determination.
The Department now proposes to extend the ﬂexibility for inc idental
teaching again through the 2026-2027 school year. This proposal will en-
able school districts to address ongoing stafﬁng challenge s by providing
them with ﬂexibility in making teaching assignments.
4. COSTS:
a. Costs to State government: The amendment does not impose a ny
costs on State government, including the State Education Department.
b. Costs to local government: The amendment does not impose a ny
costs on local government.
c. Costs to private regulated parties: The amendment does no t impose
any costs on private regulated parties.
d. Costs to regulating agency for implementation and contin ued
administration: See above.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES:
The proposed amendment does not impose any additional program, ser-
vice, duty, or responsibility upon any local government.
6. PAPERWORK:
The proposed amendment does not impose any additional paper work
requirements.
7. DUPLICA TION:
The proposed amendment does not duplicate existing State or Federal
requirements.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
The proposed amendment is necessary to extend the ﬂexibilit y for
incidental teaching through the 2026-2027 school year that enables school
districts to address ongoing stafﬁng challenges by providi ng them with
ﬂexibility in making teaching assignments. Therefore, no alternatives were
considered.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS:
There are no applicable Federal standards.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
It is anticipated that the proposed rule will be presented to the Board of
Regents for permanent adoption at its January 2027 meeting, which is the
ﬁrst scheduled meeting after expiration of the 60-day public comment pe-
riod mandated by SAPA for State agency rulemaking. However, since the
emergency action will expire before the January 2027 Regents meeting, it
is anticipated that an additional emergency action will be p resented for
adoption at the November 2026 Regents meeting. If adopted at the Janu-
ary 2027 meeting, the proposed amendment will become effect ive as a
permanent rule on January 27, 2027. Since the proposed amend ment
merely extends ﬂexibility for incidental teaching through the 2026-2027
school year, the proposed amendment does not impose any comp liance
schedules on regulated parties.

Text of rule and any required statements and analyses may be obtained
from: Joshua Dingman, NYS Education Department, Ofﬁce of Counsel ,
89 Washington Avenue, Albany, NY 12234, (518) 474-6400, ema il:
legal@nysed.gov
Initial Review of Rule
As a rule that requires a RFA, RAFA or JIS, this rule will be ini tially
reviewed in the calendar year 2029, which is no later than the 3rd year af-
ter the year in which this rule is being adopted.
Assessment of Public Comment
Since publication of the Notice of Proposed Rule Making in th e State
Register on June 3, 2026, the State Education Department rec eived the
following comment on the proposed amendment:
1. COMMENT: A New Y ork State professional association representing
speech-language pathologists stated that they strongly su pport the
proposed regulation. They believe this proposal “maintains high standards
for professional practice while removing unnecessary barr iers that can
delay or discourage qualiﬁed practitioners from entering t he New York
workforce.” The Association notes that the amendments will create a more
straightforward licensure process by recognizing supervi sed experiences
completed in accordance with the American Speech-Langauge Hearing
Association and by providing greater ﬂexibility for applicants completing
their supervised experience on a part-time basis. Addition ally, the As-
sociation states that the amendments are necessary given th e “growth in
demand” for speech-language pathology services in our state.
DEPARTMENT RESPONSE: The Department appreciates the sup-
portive comment as it works to reduce barriers to licensure while maintain-
ing high professional standards for speech-language patho logists. No
changes to the proposed rule are necessary.

Nurse Practitioners (NPs), Athletic Trainers, and Extra Class

(3)…

Section 136.9 Sudden Cardiac Arrest

its Regulatory Agenda for publication in the Register.
Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY:
Education Law § 101 charges the Department with the general manage-
ment and supervision of all public schools and all of the educational work
of the state.
Education Law § 207 grants general rule-making authority to the
Regents to carry into effect State educational laws and policies.
Education Law § 305(1) and (2) provide that the Commissioner, as chief
executive ofﬁcer of the State’s education system, with general supervision
over all schools and institutions subject to the Education L aw, or of any
statute relating to education, and responsibility for executing all educa-
tional policies of the Board of Regents.
Education § 305(42) provides that the Commissioner, in conj unction
with the Commissioner of Health, shall promulgate rules and regulations
relating to students who suffer mild traumatic brain injuri es, or concus-
sions, while receiving instruction or engaging in school sp onsored or re-
lated activities. Such regulations must require the immedi ate removal of
any student from athletic activities that is believed to hav e sustained or
who has sustained a mild traumatic brain injury; the student shall not
resume athletic activities until they have been evaluated and received writ-
ten authorization from a licensed physician or certiﬁed nurse practitioner.
Education Law § 923 provides that the Commissioner shall promulgate
rules and regulations requiring that any student displayin g signs or
symptoms of pending or increased risk of sudden cardiac arre st is im-
mediately removed from athletic activities and shall not re sume athletic
activities until they have been evaluated and received writ ten authoriza-
tion from a licensed physician or certiﬁed nurse practitioner.
Education Law § 8351, as amended by Chapter 733 of the Laws of 2023
(Chapter 733), deﬁnes “athletic trainer” as any person who is duly licensed
to perform athletic training under the supervision of a physician.
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
14

Chapter 159 of the Laws of 2026 (Chapter 159) amends the Education
Law to authorize nurse practitioners, in addition to physicians, to evaluate
and provide written authorization for students to resume athletic activities.
2. LEGISLA TIVE OBJECTIVES:
The proposed rule is consistent with the above statutory authority and is
necessary to authorize nurse practitioners to evaluate and provide written
authorization for students that are suspected of having suffered a concus-
sion or displaying signs or symptoms of pending or increased risk of sud-
den cardiac arrest during school athletic activities consistent with Chapter
159. The proposed rule is also necessary to clarify that athletic trainers are
licensed, rather than certiﬁed, Consistent with Chapter 733; to update the
term “athletic activities” to “extra class athletic activi ties;” and to update
the deﬁnition of “extra class athletic activities” to confo rm with deﬁni-
tions used in section 135.1 of the Commissioner’s regulations.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
The Concussion Management and Awareness Act (Education Law
§ 305[42]) and the Dominic Murray Sudden Cardiac Arrest Prev ention
Act (Education Law § 923) were enacted to protect students participating
in school athletics. Both laws require students who are susp ected of hav-
ing suffered a concussion or displaying signs or symptoms of pending or
increased risk of sudden cardiac arrest to be immediately re moved from
athletic activities, and students may not resume such activ ities until they
are evaluated by and receive written authorization from a ph ysician.
However, requiring evaluation and written authorization b y a physician
has created an unanticipated barrier to the resumption of athletic activities,
as most students are evaluated in urgent care centers and emergency rooms
staffed with non-physician health care providers, includi ng nurse
practitioners. Chapter 159 of the Laws of 2026 amended the Ed ucation
Law, recognizing this by authorizing nurse practitioners, in addition to
physicians, to evaluate and provide written authorization for students to
resume athletic activities.
Separately, the Legislature also amended Education Law § 83 05 to
update the practice of athletic trainers from a credentiale d to a licensed
profession.
Therefore, the Department proposes amendments to sections 136.5 and
136.9 to implement the new legislation described above.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to State government: The proposed rule does not impose any
costs on State government.
(b) Costs to local government: The proposed rule does not impose any
costs on local government.
(c) Cost to private regulated parties. The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on private regulated parties.
(d) Cost to the regulatory agency: The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on the State Education Department for implementat ion and
continued administration.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES:
The proposed rule does not impose any program service, duty, responsi-
bility, or other mandate on local governments.
6. PAPERWORK:
The proposed rule does not impose any paperwork mandates.
7. DUPLICA TION:
There are no other state or federal requirements on the subject matter of
the proposed rule. Therefore, the amendment does not duplic ate other
existing state or federal requirements.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
There are no signiﬁcant alternatives to the proposed rule and none were
considered.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS:
There are no related federal standards. Since there are no ap plicable
federal standards, the proposed rule does not exceed any minimum federal
standards for the same or similar subject areas.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
It is anticipated that the proposed rule will be presented to the Board of
Regents for permanent adoption at the January 2027 meeting. If adopted
at the January 2027 Regents meeting, the proposed amendment will
become effective as a permanent rule on January 27, 2027. It is anticipated
that the regulated parties will be able to comply with the proposed rule by
the effective date.
Regulatory Flexibility Analysis
(a) Small Businesses:
The purpose of the proposed rule is to authorize nurse practi tioners to
evaluate and provide written authorization for students that are suspected
of having suffered a concussion or displaying signs or symptoms of pend-
ing or increased risk of sudden cardiac arrest during school athletic activi-
ties, consistent with Chapter 159 of the Laws of 2026 (Chapter 159). The
proposed rule also clariﬁes that athletic trainers are lice nsed, rather than
certiﬁed, consistent with Chapter 733 of the Laws of 2023 (Chapter 733);
updates the term “athletic activities” to “extra class athl etic activities;”
and updates the deﬁnition of “extra class athletic activiti es” to conform
with deﬁnitions used in section 135.1 of the Commissioner’s regulations.
The proposed rule does not have any adverse economic impact o r
impose any reporting, record keeping or any other complianc e require-
ments on small businesses. Because it is evident from the nat ure of the
proposed amendment that it does not affect small businesses , no further
measures were needed to ascertain that fact and none were taken. Accord-
ingly, a regulatory ﬂexibility analysis for small businesses is not required
pursuant to section 202-b(3)(a) of the State Administrative Procedure Act,
and one has not been prepared.
(b) Local Governments:
1. EFFECT OF RULE:
The proposed rule applies to each of the 731 public school dis tricts in
the State, and all charter and nonpublic schools.
2. COMPLIANCE REQUIREMENTS:
The Concussion Management and Awareness Act (Education Law
§ 305[42]) and the Dominic Murray Sudden Cardiac Arrest Prev ention
Act (Education Law § 923) were enacted to protect students participating
in school athletics. Both laws require students who are susp ected of hav-
ing suffered a concussion or displaying signs or symptoms of pending or
increased risk of sudden cardiac arrest to be immediately re moved from
athletic activities, and students may not resume such activ ities until they
are evaluated by and receive written authorization from a ph ysician.
However, requiring evaluation and written authorization b y a physician
has created an unanticipated barrier to the resumption of athletic activities,
as most students are evaluated in urgent care centers and emergency rooms
staffed with non-physician health care providers, includi ng nurse
practitioners. Chapter 159 amended the Education Law, reco gnizing this
by authorizing nurse practitioners, in addition to physici ans, to evaluate
and provide written authorization for students to resume athletic activities.
Separately, the Legislature also amended Education Law § 83 05
(Chapter 733) to update the practice of athletic trainers from a credentialed
to a licensed profession.
Therefore, the Department proposes amendments to sections 136.5 and
136.9 to implement the new legislation described above.
3. PROFESSIONAL SERVICES:
The Department does not anticipate that any professional services will
be needed to comply with the proposed rule, beyond those need ed to
comply with statute.
4. COMPLIANCE COSTS:
The proposed rule does not impose any costs on local governments.
5. ECONOMIC AND TECHNOLOGICAL FEASIBILITY:
The proposed rule does not impose any additional technological require-
ments on school districts. Economic feasibility is address ed under the
Compliance Costs section above.
6. MINIMIZING ADVERSE IMPACT:
Consistent with Chapter 159, the proposed rule authorizes n urse
practitioners to evaluate and provide written authorizati on for students
that are suspected of having suffered a concussion or displa ying signs or
symptoms of pending or increased risk of sudden cardiac arre st during
school athletic activities. The proposed rule also clariﬁe s that athletic
trainers are licensed, rather than certiﬁed, consistent wi th Chapter 733;
updates the term “athletic activities” to “extra class athl etic activities;”
and updates the deﬁnition of “extra class athletic activiti es” to conform
with deﬁnitions used in section 135.1 of the Commissioner’s regulations.
It is evident that the proposed rule will not have any adverse economic
impact on local governments, therefore differing approaches as prescribed
in SAPA § 202-b(1) were not considered.
7. LOCAL GOVERNMENT PARTICIPA TION:
A copy of the proposed rule has been shared with school distri cts
through the ofﬁces of the district superintendents of each s upervisory
district in the State and with the chief school ofﬁcers of the ﬁve big city
school districts for review and comment.

required pursuant to section 202-bb(4)(a) of the State Administrative Pro-

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY:
Section 207 of the Education Law grants general rule-making authority
to the Board of Regents to carry into effect the laws and polic ies of the
State relating to education.
Section 6504 of the Education Law authorizes the Board of Regents to
supervise the admission to and regulation of the practice of the professions.
Section 6507 of the Education Law requires the Commissioner and the
Department to administer the admission to and the practice o f the
professions.
Section 6527 of the Education Law, as amended by Chapter 115 of the
laws of 2026 (Chapter 115), authorizes physicians to prescribe and order a
patient speciﬁc order or non-patient speciﬁc order to licensed pharmacists
to administer a COVID-19 immunization to a patient two years or older
and for patients 18 years and older, and removes the referenc e to the im-
munization being recommended by the Advisory Committee on I m-
munization Practices (“ACIP”).
Section 6801 of the Education Law, as amended by Chapter 115,
removes references to ACIP and changes the adult immunization schedule
posting requirement at pharmacies to require the posting of the adult
schedule as recommended by the Department of Health.
Section 6802 of the Education Law, as amended, authorizes pharmacists
to administer COVID-19 vaccines to patients ages two years or older and
removes a reference to only administering adult vaccines as recommended
by ACIP
Section 6909 of the Education Law, as amended by Chapter 115,
authorizes nurse practitioners to prescribe and order a patient speciﬁc or-
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
16

der or non-patient speciﬁc order to licensed pharmacists to administer a
COVID-19 immunization to a patient two years or older and for patients
18 years and older, and removes the reference to the immunization being
recommended by ACIP .
2. LEGISLA TIVE OBJECTIVES:
The proposed amendments are consistent with the above statu tory
authority and necessary to align the Commissioner’s regula tions with the
statutory amendments made by Chapter 115.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
Chapter 115 was enacted to ensure that access to life-saving immuniza-
tions continues unabated in New Y ork, by eliminating reliance based solely
on federal immunization recommendations. Consistent with Chapter 115,
the Department proposes to amend subdivision (a) of section 63.9 and
subdivision (a) of 64.7 to align such provisions with the statutory amend-
ments made by Chapter 115.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to State government: There are no additional costs to State
government.
(b) Costs to local government: There are no additional costs to local
government.
(c) Costs to private regulated parties: There are no additio nal costs to
private regulated parties and the proposed amendments.
(d) Costs to regulating agency for implementation and conti nued
administration of this rule: There are no additional costs to the regulating
agency.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES:
The proposed amendment relates to regulations governing im muniza-
tions to ensure New York State residents have access that doe s not rely
solely on federal recommendations, and it does not impose an y program,
service, duty, or responsibility upon local governments.
6. PAPERWORK:
The proposed amendment imposes no new reporting or other paperwork
requirements.
7. DUPLICA TION:
The proposed amendment does not duplicate other existing st ate or
federal requirements and is necessary to align with the stat utory amend-
ments made by Chapter 115.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
The proposed amendment is necessary to align the Commission er’s
regulations with Chapter 115 which ensure New Y ork State residents have
access to immunizations that do not rely solely on federal
recommendations. There are no signiﬁcant alternatives to t he proposed
amendments, and none were considered.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS:
Federal immunization recommendations are established by A CIP . To
the extent the proposed amendment exceeds any applicable mi nimum
federal standards for the same or similar subject areas, such provisions are
necessary to conform to the Commissioner’s regulations wit h State law
(Chapter 115).
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
The proposed amendment will become effective on January 27, 2027. It
is anticipated that New York State physicians, pharmacies, schools, and
regulatory bodies will be able to comply with the proposed am endments
by the effective date.

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY:
Education Law § 207 grants general rule-making authority to the
Regents to carry into effect State educational laws and policies.
Education Law § 210 empowers the Regents to register domesti c and
foreign institutions in terms of New Y ork standards, and ﬁx t he value of
degrees, diplomas and certiﬁcates issued by institutions of other states or
countries and presented to entrance to schools, colleges an d the profes-
sions in New Y ork.
Education Law § 214 provides that the institutions of the University of
the State of New Y ork shall include all secondary and higher educational
institutions which are now or may hereafter be incorporated in this state,
and such other libraries, museums, institutions, schools, organizations and
agencies for education as may be admitted to or incorporated by the
University.
Education Law § 215 empowers the Regents and the Commissioner to
visit, examine into and inspect, any institution in the Univ ersity and any
school or institution under the educational supervision of the State and
may require reports therefrom giving information as the Reg ents or the
Commissioner prescribe.
Education Law § 218 prescribes requirements for institutional power to
confer degrees in New Y ork State.
Education Law § 224 Education Law grants the Regents the auth ority
to confer by special charter to a university, college or other degree grant-
ing institution the ability to confer any degree or use, advertise or transact
business under the name university or college.
Education Law § 305(1) and (2) provide that the Commissioner, as chief
executive ofﬁcer of the State’s education system, with general supervision
over all schools and institutions subject to the Education L aw, or of any
statute relating to education, and responsibility for executing all educa-
tional policies of the Board of Regents.
2. LEGISLA TIVE OBJECTIVES:
The proposed rule is consistent with the above statutory authority and is
necessary to align degree conferral requirements with recent statutory and
regulatory changes and clarify the pathways through which students may
demonstrate eligibility for degree conferral.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
Section 3.47 of the Rules of the Board of Regents sets forth the require-
ments for earned degrees at institutions of higher educatio n in New Y ork
State, including the minimum qualiﬁcations that candidate s for degree
conferral must meet. In addition to completing a program registered by the
Department, candidates must provide evidence of a high scho ol diploma
or its substantial equivalent, as certiﬁed by the superinte ndent of schools
in the candidate’s district of residence at the time of completion. Candi-
dates beyond compulsory school age have additional pathways for degree
conferral eligibility, such as a high school equivalency diploma.
Recent statutory and regulatory changes impact what can be considered
an acceptable alternative to a high school diploma. These changes include
the following:
D In September 2022, the Board of Regents adopted Part 130 of th e
Regulations of the Commissioner of Education relating to su bstantially
equivalent instruction for nonpublic school students.
D The Legislature enacted Chapter 606 of the Laws of 2025 (Chap ter
606), which expanded eligibility for state ﬁnancial aid to g raduates of a
speciﬁc type of accredited online high school program. Spec iﬁcally,
Chapter 606, among other things, amends Education Law § 661( 4)(f) to
recognize high school diplomas earned through certain online high school
programs located within the United States, provided that the programs are
accredited in accordance with particular standards outlin ed in the Job
Corps Policy and Requirements Handbook. Chapter 606 also al lows for
admission to SUNY , CUNY , and community colleges for graduates of this
speciﬁc type of accredited online high school program, if more than 10,000
students per year nationally complete such program.
D In May 2026, Department staff proposed amendments to section 145-
2.15 of the Regulations of the Commissioner to align state ﬁn ancial aid
eligibility with Chapter 606 and clarify eligibility for st udents who
completed the substantial equivalent of a four-year high sc hool course
through home instruction pursuant to section 100.10 of the C ommis-
sioner’s regulations.
D Contemporaneously, Department staff is presenting a separate item at
this meeting containing revisions to the proposed amendmen ts to section
145-2.15 of the Commissioner’s regulations to clarify Stat e ﬁnancial aid
eligibility for students who graduated from a high school deemed to offer
substantial equivalency of instruction pursuant to Part 130 of the Commis-
sioner’s regulations.
This activity has resulted in increased inquiries to the Dep artment
regarding the degree conferral provisions in section 3.47 o f the Rules of
the Board of Regents.
The Department is proposing regulatory amendments to align section
3.47 of the Regents Rules and section 145-2.15 of the Commiss ioner’s
regulations with each other and with Chapter 606. The propos ed amend-
ments to section 3.47 adds the following options for student s to demon-
strate eligibility for degree conferral:
D Graduation from a high school deemed to offer substantial equivalency
of instruction pursuant to Part 130 of the Commissioner’s Re gulations.
However, this option does not apply to graduates of a high school deemed
to offer substantial equivalency of instruction solely under the provisions
of section 3204(6)(b)(iii) of the Education Law.
1
D For students beyond compulsory school age, possession of a h igh
school diploma, or the equivalent, from an online high school program lo-
cated within the United States that is accredited in accorda nce with
paragraph four of appendix three hundred two of the Job Corps Policy and
Requirements Handbook.
The proposed amendment also clariﬁes aspects of two current options
for students to demonstrate eligibility for degree conferr al. First, for
students seeking eligibility based upon a high school diplo ma, the
proposed language clariﬁes that the candidate must possess a New York
State diploma or the equivalent from their jurisdiction at t he time of
graduation. Second, in the option that recognizes students who complete
the substantial equivalent of a four-year high school cours e, the Depart-
ment proposed language indicating that such course must be through home
instruction pursuant to section 100.10 of the Commissioner’s regulations.
Additional options for students beyond compulsory school a ge remain

unchanged, except for a technical amendment that updates la nguage
referencing accrediting bodies to conform to other languag e in the Com-
missioner’s regulations and Regents Rules.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to State government: The proposed rule does not impose any
costs on State government.
(b) Costs to local government: The proposed rule does not impose any
costs on local government.
(c) Cost to private regulated parties. The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on private regulated parties.
(d) Cost to the regulatory agency: The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on the State Education Department for implementat ion and
continued administration.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES:
The proposed rule does not impose any program service, duty, responsi-
bility, or other mandate on local governments.
6. PAPERWORK:
The proposed rule does not impose any paperwork mandates.
7. DUPLICA TION:
There are no other state or federal requirements on the subject matter of
the proposed rule. Therefore, the amendment does not duplic ate other
existing state or federal requirements.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
The proposed rule is necessary to align degree conferral req uirements
with recent statutory and regulatory changes and clarify th e pathways
through which students may demonstrate eligibility for deg ree conferral.
There are no signiﬁcant alternatives to the proposed rule an d none were
considered.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS:
There are no related federal standards. Since there are no ap plicable
federal standards, the proposed rule does not exceed any minimum federal
standards for the same or similar subject areas.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
It is anticipated that the proposed rule will be presented to the Board of
Regents for permanent adoption at the January 2027 meeting. If adopted
at the January 2027 Regents meeting, the proposed amendment will
become effective as a permanent rule on January 27, 2027. The proposed
amendment does not impose any compliance schedules on regul ated
parties.

Revised Regulatory Impact Statement
Since publication of a Notice of Proposed Rule Making in the S tate
Register on June 3, 2026, the following substantial revisions were made to
the proposed rule:
In September 2022, the Board of Regents adopted Part 130 of th e
Regulations of the Commissioner of Education relating to su bstantially
equivalent instruction for nonpublic school students. Wit h the enactment
of Part 130, there is now a regulatory process set forth to ens ure that
students who graduate from a non-public school have received an educa-
tion that is substantially equivalent to students in their home district who
attend the public schools.
Therefore, the Department proposes to revise the proposed r ule to
clarify that a student who graduates from a high school deeme d to offer
substantial equivalency of instruction pursuant to Part 130 of the Commis-
sioner’s regulations is eligible for state ﬁnancial aid.
1 This revision is con-
sistent with amendments proposed to section 3.47 of the Rules of the Board
of Regents related to qualiﬁcations that candidates for deg ree conferral
must meet.
The above changes require that the NEEDS AND BENEFITS, COSTS,
ALTERNATIVES, and COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE sections of the
previously published Regulatory Impact Statement be revis ed to read as
follows:
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS:
The Legislature enacted Chapter 606 of the Laws of 2025 (Chap ter
606), which expanded eligibility for state ﬁnancial aid to g raduates of a
speciﬁc type of accredited online high school program. Spec iﬁcally,
Chapter 606, among other things, amends Education Law § 661( 4)(f) to
recognize high school diplomas earned through certain online high school
programs located within the United States, provided that the programs are
accredited in accordance with particular standards outlin ed in the Job
Corps Policy and Requirements Handbook.
2
Under this change, students who obtain such diplomas are eli gible to
apply for New Y ork State ﬁnancial aid programs, including Tuition Assis-
tance Program (TAP) awards. By formally recognizing high sc hool
diplomas from online high schools that are accredited under particular
federal Job Corps standards, graduates who hold such diplomas no longer
need to pass an Ability-to-Beneﬁt (ATB) test or pursue one of several
pathways for a High School Equivalency diploma.
Section 145-2.15 of the Commissioner’s regulations governs how A TB
tests are used and administered to determine eligibility for New Y ork State
student ﬁnancial aid awards under Education Law § 661. The cu rrent
regulatory language does not explicitly recognize diploma s issued by
online high school programs that meet the Job Corps accredit ation stan-
dards as a qualifying form of secondary education credentia l. Therefore,
the Department proposes to amend sections 145-2.15(c)(1) and (2) to align
the Commissioner’s Regulations with the updated statutory requirements
pursuant to Chapter 606.
Additionally, the proposed amendment clariﬁes, consisten t with the
requirements for earned degrees in section 3.47 of the Rules of the Board
of Regents, that the substantial equivalent of a four-year high school course
through home instruction pursuant to section 100.10 of the C ommis-
sioner’s regulation, as certiﬁed by the superintendent of schools or compa-
rable chief school administrator of such student’s school d istrict of resi-
dence at the time such course was completed, is sufﬁcient for the purposes
of New Y ork State student ﬁnancial aid eligibility. Although this eligibility
criterion has been permitted under the current regulations, the Department
intends to make this option transparent for students seeking ﬁnancial aid.
Revisions to the Proposed Rule
In September 2022, the Board of Regents adopted Part 130 of th e
Regulations of the Commissioner of Education relating to su bstantially
equivalent instruction for nonpublic school students. Wit h the enactment
of Part 130, there is now a regulatory process set forth to ens ure that
students who graduate from a non-public school have received an educa-
tion that is substantially equivalent to students in their home district who
attend the public schools.
Therefore, the Department proposes to revise the proposed r ule to
clarify that a student who graduates from a high school deeme d to offer
substantial equivalency of instruction pursuant to Part 130 of the Commis-
sioner’s regulations is eligible for state ﬁnancial aid.
3 This revision is con-
sistent with amendments proposed to section 3.47 of the Rules of the Board
of Regents related to qualiﬁcations that candidates for deg ree conferral
must meet.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to State government: While the proposed rule broadens the list
of eligibility for students to obtain state ﬁnancial aid to i nclude students
with a high school diploma from certain online high school pr ograms, as
well as students who have graduated from a high school deemed to offer
substantial equivalency of instruction pursuant to Part 130 of the Commis-
sioner’s regulations, the Department does not anticipate t hese amend-
ments to impose signiﬁcant costs to the State beyond those im posed by
statute, as students still have an alternate avenue to obtai n eligibility for
state ﬁnancial aid; receiving a passing score on a federally approved
ability-to-beneﬁt test.
(b) Costs to local government: The proposed rule does not impose any
costs on local government.
(c) Cost to private regulated parties: The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on private regulated parties.
(d) Cost to the regulatory agency: The proposed rule does not impose
any costs on the State Education Department for implementat ion and
continued administration.
8. ALTERNA TIVES:
The proposed rule is necessary to align the Commissioner’s R egula-
tions with the updated statutory requirements pursuant to C hapter 606.
The proposed amendment clariﬁes, consistent with the requi rements for
earned degrees in section 3.47 of the Rules of the Board of Reg ents, that
the substantial equivalent of a four-year high school course through home
instruction pursuant to section 100.10 of the Commissioner ’s regulation,
as certiﬁed by the superintendent of schools or comparable c hief school
administrator of such student’s school district of residence at the time such
course was completed, is sufﬁcient for the purposes of New Yo rk State
student ﬁnancial aid eligibility. Additionally, with the e nactment of Part
130, there is now a regulatory process set forth to ensure that students who
graduate from a non-public school have received an educatio n that is
substantially equivalent to students in their home distric t who attend the
public schools. Therefore, a student who graduates from a hi gh school
deemed to offer substantial equivalency of instruction pursuant to Part 130
of the Commissioner’s regulations is eligible for state ﬁnancial aid.
4 This
revision is consistent with amendments proposed to section 3.47 of the
Rules of the Board of Regents related to qualiﬁcations that candidates for
degree conferral must meet. There are no signiﬁcant alterna tives to the
proposed rule and none were considered.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE:
It is anticipated that the proposed rule will be presented to the Board of
Regents for permanent adoption at the December 2026 meeting. If adopted
at the December 2026 Regents meeting, the proposed amendmen t will
become effective as a permanent rule on December 31, 2026. Th e
proposed amendment does not impose any compliance schedule s on
regulated parties.

State Board of Elections
NOTICE OF ADOPTION
Providing Conﬂict of Interest Regulations for Local Board of
Elections Employees
I.D. No. SBE-10-26-00002-A
Filing No. 871
Filing Date: 2026-09-15
Effective Date: 2026-09-30
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following action:
Action taken: Addition of Part 6224 to Title 9 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Election Law, sections 3-102(17) and 3-304
Subject: Providing conﬂict of interest regulations for local board of elec-
tions employees.
Purpose: To ensure the bipartisan, standardized, transparent, appl ication
of conﬂicts of interest rules at boards of elections.
Text of ﬁnal rule: A new Part 6224 is added to Title 9 NYCRR to read as
follows:
P ART 6224. CONFLICTS OF INTEREST AMONG BOARD OF ELEC-
TIONS EMPLOYEES
Section 6224.1. Deﬁnitions
The terms used in this Part shall have the signiﬁcance herein deﬁned
unless another meaning is clearly apparent in language or content.
(a) Conﬂict of interest means a situation wherein a person is in a posi-
tion to derive pecuniary beneﬁt from actions or decisions made in their of-
ﬁcial capacity.
(b) Board of elections employee means any person directly compensated
with wages or salary by a board of elections for full-time or p art-time
work, excluding election inspectors, poll clerks, and election coordinators
compensated solely for their work at a general, primary, or s pecial
election.
(c) Direct ﬁnancial interest means a monetary beneﬁt earned by an in-
dividual or by an entity in which said individual has an ownership stake,
beneﬁcial interest, or controlling inﬂuence.
(d) V endor means any manufacturer , company, or individual who seeks
to sell voting systems and/or election-related services for such systems in
New York State, including but not limited to voting machines , electronic
poll books, printers, tabulation equipment, voter registration systems, poll
worker management systems, election night reporting syste ms, or any
other technical or electronic equipment utilized in election administration.
For purposes of subdivision (b) of this section, vendor shal l also mean
printing companies, election consulting companies, direct mail companies
and digital marketing companies.
(e) Party position means an unpaid position within a recognized politi-
cal party, including county committee member , district leader , State com-
mittee member , chair of a political party, member of an executive commit-
tee for a political party, delegate or alternate delegate to a nominating or
party convention or judicial convention, or any similar internal party of-
ﬁce that does not carry compensation. Party positions shall not be
considered public ofﬁces for purposes of this Part.
Section 6224.2. Prohibition of conﬂicts of interest
(a) No board of elections employee shall engage in or partici pate in
any trade or business which creates, or may tend to create, an actual or
potential conﬂict of interest.
(b) No board of elections employee shall maintain a direct ﬁn ancial
interest in or be employed by a vendor or a company providing services to
a candidate who has an election overseen by such employee’s o fﬁce,
including but not limited to, printing companies, election consulting
companies, direct mail companies and digital marketing companies.
(c) No board of elections employee shall maintain a direct ﬁn ancial
interest in or be employed by a vendor or company that sells to the board
voting machines, electronic poll books, printers, tabulation equipment, or
other technical or electronic equipment.
(d) No board of elections employee shall remain on the board o f elec-
tions payroll while also a candidate for an ofﬁce who has an el ection
overseen, in whole or in part, by the board at which they are em ployed,
without prior authorization by a majority vote of the board o f elections
commissioners and in accordance with the provisions of section 6224.3 of
this Part.
Section 6224.3. Authorization for employee to seek elective ofﬁce
(a) A board of elections employee who is also a candidate for public of-
ﬁce at an election overseen by said board may remain under paid employ-
ment under the following circumstances:
(1) The commissioners of the board of elections approve the authori-
zation by majority vote, and issue said authorization in a si gned writing
executed by both commissioners and acknowledged by the employee ask-
ing for such approval. The result of any such vote shall be made public.
(2) A board of elections employee so authorized shall be rest ricted
from working on, and must not have any material contact with, or decision-
making authority as to matters related to the election in the political
subdivision in which the covered employee is a candidate, in cluding but
not limited to:
(i) designating or nominating petitions which the covered employ-
ee’ s name appears as a candidate for public ofﬁce or any other petitions
for any other person seeking the same ofﬁce in the same political subdivi-
sion;
(ii) canvass of returns of elections in the same political subdivision
as the covered employee, election day operations, poll site management,
poll worker assignments or related election management act ivities in the
same political subdivision where the covered employee’ s name appears as
a candidate for public ofﬁce;
(iii) voting machine setup, maintenance, testing, or any re lated
activities for machines in use in the same political subdivi sion where the
covered employee’ s name appears as a candidate for public ofﬁce;
(iv) voter registration activities for any voter in the poli tical
subdivision where the covered employee’ s name appears as a c andidate
for public ofﬁce; or
(v) any other board activity wherein participation or action by the
covered employee could create the appearance of impropriety or a conﬂict
of interest.
(b) A board of elections employee so authorized shall be proh ibited
from soliciting or accepting any contribution, as deﬁned in section 14-
100(9) of the Election Law, from any other employee of said bo ard of
elections.
(c) A board of elections employee shall be deemed a candidate for elec-
tive ofﬁce upon the ﬁling of a designating or nominating petition for such
ofﬁce or , where nominations for such ofﬁce are made other tha n by peti-
tion, upon acceptance of a nomination.
(d) Notwithstanding the provisions of this Part, when there are no pri-
mary elections for the ofﬁce being sought by a candidate that is also a
board of elections employee, that candidate may remain on th e board of
elections payroll until 90 days prior to a general election.
(e) A board of elections employee who either does not request authori-
zation or was denied authorization under this section shall be placed in
‘ ‘leave without pay’ ’ status until such time as their candidacy shall cease,
or upon the day following the certiﬁcation of election results for such of-
ﬁce, whichever comes ﬁrst.
(f) The provisions of this section shall not apply to unpaid p arty
positions.
Section 6224.4. Penalties
(a) Violation of these provisions shall be cause for discipl ine by the
board of elections. Penalties for violation of these provisions may include,
but are not limited to:
(1) Suspension of the board of elections employee without pa y, with
said suspension not to be lifted until the conﬂict of interest is remedied.
NYS Register/September 30, 2026 Rule Making Activities
21

(2) Termination of employment.
(3) Referral of the board of elections employee to local law
enforcement.
Section 6224.5. Endorsements not to create conﬂicts of interest
(a) It shall not be considered a conﬂict of interest for a boar d of elec-
tions employee to seek and/or serve in a party position, or to endorse or
support a candidate for a party position.
(b) It shall not be deemed a conﬂict of interest for a board of elections
employee to endorse or support a candidate for public ofﬁce when acting
solely in their capacity as a county committee member, distr ict leader,
State committee member , chair of a political party, member o f an execu-
tive committee for a political party, delegate or alternate delegate to a
nominating or party convention or judicial convention.
(c) Nothing in this section shall be construed to permit othe rwise
impermissible political activity within a board of elections.

were made in 6224.2(d), 6224.3(a)(1) and 6224.5.
