NOTICE OF ADOPTION
Ballot Drop Boxes
I.D. No. SBE-24-26-00025-A
Filing No. 875
Filing Date: 2026-09-15
Effective Date: 2026-09-30
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following action:
Action taken: Addition of Part 6225 to Title 9 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Election Law, sections 3-102(1), (17) and 8-416(3)
Subject: Ballot drop boxes.
Purpose: Provides procedures and requirements for ballot drop boxes.
Substance of ﬁnal rule: This proposed Part 6225 to 9 NYCRR establishes
statewide guidelines for counties that choose to use ballot return drop
boxes (excluding polling places and boards of elections ofﬁces).
Drop box design / voter-facing requirements:
D Outdoor unstaffed boxes must be durable, resistant to vanda lism/
removal/weather, and include ﬁre-mitigation features (re ferencing CISA
best practices).
D Slot must allow deposit but prevent removal/tampering; minimize liq-
uid intrusion and prevent rain/snow entry if unsheltered.
D Must be tamper-evident (seals permitted), clearly marked “ Ofﬁcial
Ballot Drop Box,” and include required multilingual signage (felony tam-
pering warning; no postage; contact info for problems/full /damaged box;
no ballots accepted after 9:00 p.m. Election Day; ballots-only; other New
Y ork State Board of Elections-required information).
D Usability/accessibility speciﬁcations include low-force operation ( 8
5 lbs), no tight grasping/twisting, wheelchair reach range (15–48 inches)
with limited exceptions, and high-contrast/non-glare signage.
D Counties should use uniform designs within the county for unstaffed
boxes (and separately for staffed boxes).
Location decisions and deadlines:
D County board of election (BOE) chooses locations consideri ng
population/geography, convenience, transit access, community sites, secu-
rity, and funding.
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
22

D Locations must be designated by March 15; locations shared with the
State Board of Elections (SBOE).
Accessibility of sites:
D Drop-off locations must be accessible to voters with disabilities.
D If multiple boxes at one site, at least one must be accessible and inac-
cessible boxes must direct voters to the accessible one.
D Encourages placement along accessible paths from nearby tr ansit
stops, parking (including van-accessible space), and pass enger drop-off
zones; indoor sites assessed like polling places.
Security and surveillance:
D Unstaffed outdoor boxes must be anchored and placed in well- lit ar-
eas; indoor unstaffed boxes must be secured against removal.
D Staffed boxes must be secured/inaccessible when not in use.
D All drop boxes must have video surveillance (or internal cam era)
capable of capturing usable images; third-party systems allowed if access
is arranged in writing prior to placement.
D Existing boxes in use as of the 2025 general election may continue at
the same location until December 31, 2028 despite the new requirements.
Public notice, availability, and retrieval frequency:
D BOE must provide SBOE and post locations online at least 30 da ys
before the election, including staffed-box hours and acces sibility info;
updates must be provided to SBOE the same day they occur.
D Boxes must be locked/covered (or otherwise unavailable) un til the
BOE begins issuing paper ballots.
D Prior to their use drop boxes must be inspected to conﬁrm functional-
ity and emptiness.
D Ballots must be retrieved at least once each business day while boxes
are open.
D All boxes must be secured at poll close on Election day and bal lots
retrieved election night similar to poll-site returns.
D Signage must warn that voter intimidation/duress at drop bo xes is a
crime.
Chain of custody:
D At least two bipartisan designated ballot retrievers, with ID badges
and the required poll-worker oath.
D Only designated retrievers (or authorized law enforcement where
used) may transport ballots.
D On arrival, retrievers complete a retrieval form noting dro p box
location/unique ID and arrival time.
D Ballots are removed and either placed into a secure ballot transfer de-
vice (which must be sealed and seal numbers recorded) or the secure bal-
lot container (or staffed box serving as the container) is re trieved. If bal-
lots are transferred into a device, retrievers record the nu mber of ballots
moved and conﬁrm the drop box is empty.
D If the box uses an internal secure container, an empty contai ner is
placed back in the drop box before leaving—except after the ﬁnal Election
Day retrieval.
D Retrievers reseal the drop box and record new seal numbers; a fter
ﬁnal retrieval post–poll closing, the box should be locked/ covered to
prevent further deposits.
D Departure time is recorded; arrival time at the BOE/receivi ng/
processing site (or other custody-transfer point) is recorded.
D BOE (or bipartisan designees) inspect for tampering and acc ept
custody by signing the retrieval form.
D The retrieval form must be attached to the container or maintained so
it is traceable to that container and documents all custody transfers.
D When the container is opened, the BOE records the number of ballots
in it; ballot totals by drop box must be reported to SBOE per Op erations
Unit procedures.
D Retrieval forms may be electronic if the container can be matched to
the electronic record and signatures are captured/retained.
D Drop-box ballots are processed the same as ballots personal ly
delivered to the BOE.
Record retention:
D Records must be retained like other election records.
D The BOE shall take reasonable measures to request that surveillance
video of drop box locations in the possession of third partie s is retained
and accessible for at least thirty days.
Final rule as compared with last published rule: Nonsubstantial changes
were made in section 6225.6(e).
Text of rule and any required statements and analyses may be obtained
from: Brian Quail, New York State Board of Elections, 40 North Pear l
Street, Floor 5, Albany, New York 12207-2729, (518) 474-206 3, email:
brian.quail@elections.ny.gov

Analysis, Revised Rural Area Flexibility Analysis and Revi sed Job
Impact Statement

Area Flexibility Analysis and Job Impact Statement are not being submit-

Regulatory Impact Statement, Regulatory Flexibility Analysis, Rural Area
Flexibility Analysis and Job Impact Statement.
Initial Review of Rule
As a rule that requires a RFA, RAFA or JIS, this rule will be ini tially
reviewed in the calendar year 2029, which is no later than the 3rd year af-
ter the year in which this rule is being adopted.
Assessment of Public Comment
The State Board of Elections received several comments regarding the
proposed addition of 9 NYCRR Part 6225. The comments general ly
expressed opposition to the use of ballot return drop boxes or commented
on their security, cost, necessity, and administration. Co mmenters argued
that New York voters already have sufﬁcient, secure methods to cast or
return ballots. Commenters also recommended additional re quirements
concerning increased drop box surveillance, bipartisan re trieval and
transportation, greater chain-of-custody documentation , ballot reconcilia-
tion procedures, more detailed incident reporting, record retention, and
public transparency.
The State Board has considered these comments and determine d that
the existing regulatory framework, together with applicab le statutory
requirements appropriately establish requirements for th e administration
of ballot drop boxes. Accordingly, no changes have been made in response
to these comments.

Licensing of Owners in Thoroughbred Racing
I.D. No. SGC-39-26-00023-P
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following proposed rule:
Proposed Action: Amendment of section 4026.3 of Title 9 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law,
sections 104(1), (19) and 220(1)
Subject: Licensing of owners in Thoroughbred racing.
Purpose: To establish criteria for licensing horse owners in Thoroug bred
racing.
Text of proposed rule: § 4026.3. Nonmanaging ownership.
* * *
(b) Nonmanaging owners—licensing. No horse shall be entere d or
started by such horse’s managing owner that is owned by [35] 20 or [less]
fewer owners, unless all of the owners are licensed, or, if owned by more
than [35] 20 owners, unless each individual having a [three] ﬁve percent or
greater property interest is licensed as an owner by the commission. In ad-
dition, each such owner that is not an individual must be regi stered and
licensed with the commission, and all such entity’s ofﬁcers , directors,
trustees or other managing individuals must be licensed by t he commis-
sion unless otherwise directed by the commission. The commi ssion may
also require any other individual involved in ownership to b e licensed at
any time [it] the commission may deem desirable. The commission or the
State steward, for good cause, may waive any of such requirements for a
race. In no event, however, may a horse start, if the license of any such in-
dividual has been denied, suspended or revoked, until the co mmission is
satisﬁed that any withdrawal of such individual or transfer of such individ-
ual’s interest is in good faith.

obtained from: Kristen Buckley, New York State Gaming Commission,
354 Broadway Center, PO Box 7500, Schenectady, NY 12305, (518) 388-
3332, email: gamingrules@gaming.ny.gov

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY: The New Y ork State Gaming Commis-
sion (“Commission”) is authorized to promulgate the propos ed rule by
Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law (“Racing Law ”) Sec-
tions 104(1) and (19) and 220(1).
Subdivision (1) of Section 104 confers upon the Commission g eneral
jurisdiction over all gaming activities within the State an d over the
corporations, associations and persons engaged in such activities. Subdivi-
sion (19) of Section 104 authorizes the Commission to promul gate any
rules and regulations that it deems necessary to carry out it s
responsibilities.
Subdivision (1) of Section 220 authorizes the Commission to regulate
licensing of participants in Thoroughbred racing.
2. LEGISLATIVE OBJECTIVES: To enable the Commission to
enhance the integrity and safety of Thoroughbred horse racing and gener-
ate reasonable revenue for the support of government.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS: This rule making proposes to adjust th e
criteria under which non-managing owners of horses in Thoro ughbred
racing are required to be licensed by the Gaming Commission. Currently,
each non-managing owner must be licensed when there are 35 or fewer
owners and when an owner has a three percent or more interest, when
there are more than 35 owners. This proposal would change tho se
thresholds to, instead, require licensure of each owner only when there are
20 or fewer owners or an owner has a ﬁve percent or more interest, when
there are more than 20 owners. The ﬁve-percent threshold is modeled after
the Association of Racing Commissioners International Model Rule and is
a standard that many other racing jurisdictions, such as Ken tucky, Mary-
land and Ohio, use. Adjusting the threshold for licensing is expected to
reduce administrative burden without compromising the int egrity of
racing.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to regulated parties for the implementation and co ntinuing
compliance with the rule: None.
(b) Costs to the regulating agency, the State, and local governments for
the implementation and continued administration of the rule: No additional
operating costs are anticipated as a result of the proposed rule.
(c) The information, including the source or sources of such informa-
tion, and methodology upon which the cost estimate is based: The cost
estimates are based on the Commission’s experience with reg ulating
Thoroughbred racing.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT: None. The Commission is the only
governmental entity authorized to regulate Thoroughbred racing activities.
6. PAPERWORK: The proposed rule would reduce paperwork
requirements.
7. DUPLICATION: The proposed regulation does not duplicate any
existing State or federal requirements of the same or similar subject matter.
8. ALTERNA TIVES: The Commission could continue the current rules
or employ different thresholds for licensure. The threshold used in the As-
sociation of Racing Commissioners International Model Rule was deemed
to be sufﬁcient.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS: There are no federal standards applicable
to this aspect of Thoroughbred racing.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE: The Commission anticipates that af-
fected parties will be able to achieve compliance with these rules upon
publication of the Notice of Adoption in the State Register.
Regulatory Flexibility Analysis, Rural Area Flexibility Analysis and Job
Impact Statement
The proposed rule making will amend the rules governing the c ircum-
stances under which non-managing owners require a license f rom the
Gaming Commission. These amendments will not have any adver se
impact on small businesses, local governments, jobs or rural areas.
The proposed rulemaking does not affect local governments a nd will
not harm small businesses in New Y ork State.
When adopted, the rule will apply uniformly throughout the State, and
it will impose no adverse impact on rural areas.
This rule making will not limit or decrease job opportunities.
Because the proposed rulemaking will not adversely impact small busi-
nesses, local governments, jobs or rural areas, a full Regulatory Flexibility
Analysis, Rural Area Flexibility Analysis and Job Impact St atement are
not required and have not been prepared.
PROPOSED RULE MAKING
NO HEARING(S) SCHEDULED
Coupling in Thoroughbred Racing
I.D. No. SGC-39-26-00024-P
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following proposed rule:
Proposed Action: Amendment of section 4025.10 of Title 9 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law,
sections 104(1), (19) and 235(1)
Subject: Coupling in Thoroughbred racing.
Purpose: To enhance wagering interest in Thorougbred racing.
Text of proposed rule: Section 4025.10 of 9 NYCRR would be amended
to read as follows:
§ 4025.10. Limitations on entries.
* * *
(b) Coupling.
(1) Horses that have the same individual present in the manag ing
ownership of the horses, that share a 25 percent commonality in owner-
ship or that share a common trainer are related horses for purposes of this
section.
(2) Related horses [may] shall not be coupled as a single betting
interest in a race, unless paragraph [(6)] (3) of this subdivision applies.
(3) [If more than two horses in a race are related horses, such horses
shall be coupled as a single betting interest in such race, unless paragraph
(6) of this subdivision applies.
(4)] The State steward may require any related horses entere d in a
race to be coupled as a single betting interest in a race prior to the com-
mencement of wagering on-track and off-track, if such stewa rd, in such
steward’s discretion, ﬁnds it necessary in the public interest.
[(5)] (4) If related horses are not coupled, the racetrack operator shall
take such actions as are necessary to inform the public adequ ately with
regard to the common ownership and/or trainer [that would ot herwise
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
24

make] of such horses [eligible to be coupled as a single betting inter est
pursuant to this subdivision].
[(6) No entry shall be coupled by reason of common ownership o r
training in any stakes race in which the gross purse is $50,00 0 or more,
provided, however, that paragraph (4) of this subdivision s hall apply in
any such race. In any race subject to the provisions of this paragraph, the
racing secretary shall have the authority to establish a mut uel ﬁeld and
coupled entries in any race with more than 14 starters.
(7)] (5) All horses trained or ridden by a spouse, parent, issue or

[(c) Entries if related horses would exclude another horse. Up to two re-

Text of proposed rule and any required statements and analyses may be
obtained from: Kristen Buckley, New York State Gaming Commission,
354 Broadway Center, PO Box 7500, Schenectady, NY 12305, (518) 388-
3332, email: gamingrules@gaming.ny.gov
Data, views or arguments may be submitted to: Same as above.
Public comment will be received until: 60 days after publication of this
notice.
This rule was not under consideration at the time this agency submitted
its Regulatory Agenda for publication in the Register.
Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY: The New Y ork State Gaming Commis-
sion (“Commission”) is authorized to promulgate the propos ed rule by
Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law (“Racing Law ”) Sec-
tions 104(1), (19) and 235(1).
Subdivision (1) of Section 104 confers upon the Commission g eneral
jurisdiction over all gaming activities within the State an d over the
corporations, associations and persons engaged in such activities. Subdivi-
sion (19) of Section 104 authorizes the Commission to promul gate any
rules and regulations that it deems necessary to carry out it s
responsibilities.
Subdivision (1) of Section 235 authorizes the Commission to make
rules regulating the conduct of pari-mutuel wagering.
2. LEGISLATIVE OBJECTIVES: To enable the Commission to
enhance the integrity of Thoroughbred horse racing and gene rate reason-
able revenue for the support of government.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS: This rule making proposes to amend the
Commission’s regulations to not require coupling of horses for wagering
purposes unless the State steward ﬁnds it necessary to do so in the public
interest. Coupling is the combining of two or more horses in a race into a
single betting interest, so that a bettor who wagers on such i nterest wins
the bet if any of the coupled horses satisﬁes the bet condition (for example,
to win, place or show). Historically, when horses had common ownership
or a common trainer, coupling was thought to protect bettors on a weaker
horse in the group, because the common owner or trainer might set up the
race strategy to sacriﬁce the interest of the weaker horse in the grouping to
the beneﬁt of a stronger horse in the grouping. By requiring c oupling,
regulators permit the bettor to win a bet if any horse in the grouping satis-
ﬁes the bet condition. Y et coupling requirements also reduc e the number
of betting interests in a race, and races with fewer betting interests are less
appealing to the wagering public, thereby reducing revenue opportunity
for the betting platforms and, indirectly, to government.
Regulated parties, including the New York Racing Associati on, Inc.,
Finger Lakes Racetrack, The New York Thoroughbred Horsemen ’s As-
sociation and The New Y ork Thoroughbred Breeders, Inc., have expressed
to Commission staff that they support the elimination of cou pling rules.
Those supporting elimination contend that loosening coupling rules would
increase betting interest and handle. They state that less r estrictive
coupling rules have been implemented successfully in other racing
jurisdictions, such as California, Illinois, Kentucky, New Jersey, Maryland
and others, and that race-integrity concerns can be addressed as they arise
through close analysis of stored video ﬁles of races. For exa mple, the
Commission promulgated the current Rule 4025.10(g) in 2015 , which
eliminated mandatory coupling in any stakes race with a purse of $50,000
or more.
In 2025, the Commission continued with its measured approac h in
amending the coupling rules by limiting the number of uncoupled entries
with common ties in races to no more than two, continuing to em power
the State steward to exercise discretion to couple when in the public inter-
est, requiring the wager acceptor to provide adequate notice to the betting
public concerning such entries and maintaining the current rule that limits
the number (at two) of horses trained by the same trainer that may be
drawn into a race to the exclusion of another horse. Commissi on staff
studied the effect of the 2025 rule amendment and identiﬁed no systemic
integrity risk to racing as a result of the change in coupling requirements.
The Commission believes that adoption of this rule making, by relaxing
the requirement to couple horses, will enhance the ability t o offer races
with more betting interest, which will be appealing to the wagering public
and enhance revenue, while continuing to protect bettors by requiring
disclosure of related horses to the public. The State steward would retain
the discretion to require coupling in any circumstances in w hich such
steward concludes coupling is necessary in the public inter est, which
should continue to protect the interests of the wagering public in unusual
or unforeseen circumstances in a particular race.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to regulated parties for the implementation and co ntinuing
compliance with the rule: None. No costs are imposed on any re gulated
entity as a result of the proposal.
(b) Costs to the regulating agency, the State, and local governments for
the implementation and continued administration of the rule: No additional
operating costs are anticipated as a result of the proposed rule.
(c) The information, including the source or sources of such informa-
tion, and methodology upon which the cost estimate is based: The cost
estimates are based on the Commission’s experience with reg ulating
Thoroughbred racing.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT: None. The Commission is the only
governmental entity authorized to regulate Thoroughbred racing activities.
6. PAPERWORK: The proposed rule imposes no changes in paperwork
requirements.
7. DUPLICATION: The proposed regulation does not duplicate any
existing State or federal requirements of the same or similar subject matter.
8. ALTERNA TIVES: The Commission could continue the current rules.
This alternative was rejected because the proposed rule wou ld more ef-
fectively increase the number of betting interests in a race , and thereby
potentially increase betting handle, while continuing to promote transpar-
ency of information to the betting public without compromising the integ-
rity of the races.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS: There are no federal standards applicable
to this aspect of Thoroughbred racing.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE: The Commission anticipates that af-
fected parties will be able to achieve compliance with these rules upon
publication of the Notice of Adoption in the State Register.

Regulatory Impact Statement
1. STA TUTORY AUTHORITY: The New Y ork State Gaming Commis-
sion (“Commission”) is authorized to promulgate the propos ed rule by
Racing, Pari-Mutuel Wagering and Breeding Law (“Racing Law ”) Sec-
tions 104(1) and (19) and 235(1).
Subdivision (1) of Section 104 confers upon the Commission g eneral
jurisdiction over all gaming activities within the State an d over the
corporations, associations and persons engaged in such activities. Subdivi-
sion (19) of Section 104 authorizes the Commission to promul gate any
rules and regulations that it deems necessary to carry out it s
responsibilities.
Subdivision (1) of Section 235 authorizes the Commission to make
rules regulating the conduct of pari-mutuel wagering.
2. LEGISLATIVE OBJECTIVES: To enable the Commission to
enhance the integrity of Thoroughbred horse racing and gene rate reason-
able revenue for the support of government.
3. NEEDS AND BENEFITS: This rule making proposes to amend the
Commission’s regulations to not require coupling of horses for wagering
purposes unless the State steward ﬁnds it necessary to do so in the public
interest. Coupling is the combining of two or more horses in a race into a
single betting interest, so that a bettor who wagers on such i nterest wins
the bet if any of the coupled horses satisﬁes the bet condition (for example,
to win, place or show). Historically, when horses had common ownership
or a common trainer, coupling was thought to protect bettors on a weaker
horse in the group, because the common owner or trainer might set up the
race strategy to sacriﬁce the interest of the weaker horse in the grouping to
the beneﬁt of a stronger horse in the grouping. By requiring c oupling,
regulators permit the bettor to win a bet if any horse in the grouping satis-
ﬁes the bet condition. Y et coupling requirements also reduc e the number
of betting interests in a race, and races with fewer betting interests are less
appealing to the wagering public, thereby reducing revenue opportunity
for the betting platforms and, indirectly, to government.
Regulated parties, including the New York Racing Associati on, Inc.,
Finger Lakes Racetrack, The New York Thoroughbred Horsemen ’s As-
sociation and The New Y ork Thoroughbred Breeders, Inc., have expressed
to Commission staff that they support the elimination of cou pling rules.
Those supporting elimination contend that loosening coupling rules would
increase betting interest and handle. They state that less r estrictive
coupling rules have been implemented successfully in other racing
jurisdictions, such as California, Illinois, Kentucky, New Jersey, Maryland
and others, and that race-integrity concerns can be addressed as they arise
through close analysis of stored video ﬁles of races. For exa mple, the
Commission promulgated the current Rule 4025.10(g) in 2015 , which
eliminated mandatory coupling in any stakes race with a purse of $50,000
or more.
In 2025, the Commission continued with its measured approac h in
amending the coupling rules by limiting the number of uncoupled entries
with common ties in races to no more than two, continuing to em power
the State steward to exercise discretion to couple when in the public inter-
est, requiring the wager acceptor to provide adequate notice to the betting
public concerning such entries and maintaining the current rule that limits
the number (at two) of horses trained by the same trainer that may be
drawn into a race to the exclusion of another horse. Commissi on staff
studied the effect of the 2025 rule amendment and identiﬁed no systemic
integrity risk to racing as a result of the change in coupling requirements.
The Commission believes that adoption of this rule making, by relaxing
the requirement to couple horses, will enhance the ability t o offer races
with more betting interest, which will be appealing to the wagering public
and enhance revenue, while continuing to protect bettors by requiring
disclosure of related horses to the public. The State steward would retain
the discretion to require coupling in any circumstances in w hich such
steward concludes coupling is necessary in the public inter est, which
should continue to protect the interests of the wagering public in unusual
or unforeseen circumstances in a particular race.
4. COSTS:
(a) Costs to regulated parties for the implementation and co ntinuing
compliance with the rule: None. No costs are imposed on any re gulated
entity as a result of the proposal.
(b) Costs to the regulating agency, the State, and local governments for
the implementation and continued administration of the rule: No additional
operating costs are anticipated as a result of the proposed rule.
(c) The information, including the source or sources of such informa-
tion, and methodology upon which the cost estimate is based: The cost
estimates are based on the Commission’s experience with reg ulating
Thoroughbred racing.
5. LOCAL GOVERNMENT MANDA TES: None. The Commission is
the only governmental entity authorized to regulate Thorou ghbred racing
activities.
6. PAPERWORK: The proposed rule imposes no changes in paperwork
requirements.
7. DUPLICATION: The proposed regulation does not duplicate any
existing State or federal requirements of the same or similar subject matter.
8. ALTERNA TIVES: The Commission could continue the current rules.
This alternative was rejected because the proposed rule wou ld more ef-
fectively increase the number of betting interests in a race , and thereby
potentially increase betting handle, while continuing to promote transpar-
ency of information to the betting public without compromising the integ-
rity of the races.
9. FEDERAL STANDARDS: There are no federal standards applicable
to this aspect of Thoroughbred racing.
10. COMPLIANCE SCHEDULE: The Commission anticipates that af-
fected parties will be able to achieve compliance with these rules upon
publication of the Notice of Adoption in the State Register.
Regulatory Flexibility Analysis, Rural Area Flexibility Analysis and Job
Impact Statement
The proposed rule making will amend the rules governing the c ircum-
stances under which horses must be coupled as a single betting interest in
Thoroughbred horse racing. These amendments will not have any adverse
impact on small businesses, local governments, jobs or rural areas.
The proposed rulemaking does not affect local governments a nd will
not harm small businesses in New Y ork State.
When adopted, the rule will apply uniformly throughout the State, and
it will impose no adverse impact on rural areas.
This rule making will not limit or decrease job opportunities.
Because the proposed rulemaking will not adversely impact small busi-
nesses, local governments, jobs or rural areas, a full Regulatory Flexibility
Analysis, Rural Area Flexibility Analysis and Job Impact St atement are
not required and have not been prepared.

Consensus Rule Making Determination
This proposed rulemaking will allow Thoroughbred racetracks to direct
patrons to relevant information available on websites, in l ieu of posting
the information physically at the track; eliminate an obsolete ﬁling require-
ment related to an era when totalisators were not always used ; and allow
race entries to be made electronically. These changes would modernize
how information is required to be provided and would not inconvenience
the public or regulated parties. An obsolete reference to th e State Tax
Commission would be updated.
Because this rule making would modernize the methods by whic h in-
formation is provided, the Commission does not anticipate p ublic com-
ment and no person is likely to object to the proposed revisions.
Job Impact Statement
A Job Impact Statement is not required for this consensus rul emaking
proposal because the proposed amendments will not adversely affect jobs
or employment opportunities.
This proposed rulemaking will modernize methods of providi ng infor-
mation to bettors about wagering rules, eliminate an obsole te ﬁling
requirement related to an era when totalisators were not always used; and
allow race entries to be made electronically. An obsolete re ference to the
State Tax Commission would be updated.
The proposed amendments will not have an impact on jobs or employ-
ment opportunities.

Department of Labor
PROPOSED RULE MAKING
NO HEARING(S) SCHEDULED
Prevention of Retail Workplace Violence
I.D. No. LAB-39-26-00022-P
PURSUANT TO THE PROVISIONS OF THE State Administrative Pro-
cedure Act, NOTICE is hereby given of the following proposed rule:
Proposed Action: Addition of Subpart 27-2 to Title 12 NYCRR.
Statutory authority: Labor Law, sections 27-e(6) and 21(11)
Subject: Prevention of Retail Workplace Violence.
Purpose: To promulgate regulations pursuant to NYS Labor Law section
27-e(6) regarding preventing retail workplace violence.
Text of proposed rule: A new Subpart 27-2 is added to Part 27 of
Subchapter A of Chapter 1 of Title 12 of NYCRR as follows:
Subpart 27-2 – Prevention of Retail Workplace Violence
Section 27-2.1
(a) Deﬁnitions
(1) Easily Accessible. Any location that retail employees can quickly
access in the event of an emergency. A location is not easily accessible if it
is behind a locked door or other access point and retail employees cannot
access it quickly.
(2) Employer . Any person, entity, business, corporation, partnership,
limited liability company, or an association employing at l east 10 retail
employees. The term employer shall not include the State, an y political
subdivision of the State, a public authority, or any other go vernmental
agency or instrumentality.
(3) Request for Immediate Assistance. A request, such as a notice or
alert, transmitted immediately to a security ofﬁcer , manager , or supervisor
that notiﬁes them of a need for immediate assistance.
(4) Retail Employee. Any employee working at a retail store f or an
employer .
NYS Register/September 30, 2026 Rule Making Activities
27

(5) Retail Store. Any store that sells consumer commodities at retail
and which is not primarily engaged in the sale of food for consumption on
the premises.
(6) Security Ofﬁcer . Any person who is either an employee of a n
employer or a third-party whose job duties include deterren ce, observa-
tion, detection and/or reporting of incidents in order to prevent any unlaw-
ful or unauthorized activity including but not limited to unlawful or unau-
thorized intrusion or entry, larceny, vandalism, abuse, ar son or trespass
on property.
(7) Silent Response Button. A physical device or mobile phone-based
application that allows retail employees to silently request immediate as-
sistance from a security ofﬁcer , manager , or supervisor whi le working at
the employee’ s location in the case of an emergency. A silent response but-
ton may be:
(i) a device installed in an easily accessible location in the work-
place;
(ii) a wearable device; or
(iii) a mobile phone-based application.
A silent response button must be speciﬁcally designed to silently request
immediate assistance in the case of an emergency, as disting uished from
buttons, devices, or applications designed for general com munication,
such as mobile or desk phones, handheld transceivers (walkie-talkies), or
all-purpose email applications.
(8) Workplace. Any location away from an employee’ s domicile, per-
manent or temporary, where an employee performs any work-related duty
in the course of their employment by an employer .
(b) Employer Responsibilities
(1) Every employer of 500 or more retail employees statewide shall
provide every retail employee with a functioning silent response button to
request immediate assistance from a security ofﬁcer , manager , or supervi-
sor while the employee is working at the employer’ s location in the case of
an emergency.
(2) Multiple employees can be directed to utilize the same si lent re-
sponse button if that silent response button is in a location in the workplace
that is easily accessible by each employee to whom the silent response
button has been provided.
(3) An employer can fulﬁll their responsibility to provide every retail
employee with a silent response button by installing multip le devices in
easily accessible locations throughout a workplace. Each i ndividual de-
vice location does not need to be easily accessible to every e mployee, so
long as at least one device location is easily accessible to every employee
throughout their shift.
(c) Restrictions on mobile phone-based and wearable silent response
buttons.
(1) Mobile phone-based and wearable silent response button s may
only be used to track employee locations when the silent response button
has been triggered. The mobile phone-based or wearable sile nt response
button must stop tracking employee locations once the emerg ency that
caused the button to be triggered has ended.
(2) Mobile phone-based silent response buttons may only be installed
on employer-provided equipment. An employer may not requir e an em-
ployee to install a mobile phone-based silent response butt on on the em-
ployee’ s personal mobile phone or any other personal device.
(d) Instructions. Employers must provide all employees wit h written
instructions on how to use the silent response button(s) tha t they have
provided to employees.
(e) Retaliation. No employer shall retaliate against an emp loyee who
triggers a silent response button if such employee triggered the button ac-
cidentally or in good faith, even if there is ultimately no emergency to be
resolved.
Text of proposed rule and any required statements and analyses may be
obtained from: Andrew Schutts, Department of Labor, 1200 Washington
Ave., Building 12, Suite 509, (518) 485-2191, email:
regulations@labor.ny.gov
Data, views or arguments may be submitted to: Same as above.
Public comment will be received until: 60 days after publication of this
notice.
This rule was not under consideration at the time this agency submitted
its Regulatory Agenda for publication in the Register.
Regulatory Impact Statement
Labor Law Section 27-e(6) authorizes the Commissioner of La bor
(Commissioner) to, “adopt rules and regulations necessary to implement
the provisions of this section.” Labor Law Section 27-e(5) requires private
employers with 500 or more retail employees in New York State to,
“provide every retail employee with a silent response button to request im-
mediate assistance from a security ofﬁcer, manager, or supe rvisor while
the employee is working at the employee’s location in case of an
emergency.” These regulations provide necessary information to employ-
ers to meet the requirements of Labor Law Section 27-e(5).
Labor Law Section 21(11) authorizes the commissioner to issue regula-
tions governing any provision of the Labor Law as they ﬁnd necessary and
proper.
Legislative Objectives: The purpose of Labor Law Section 27 -e is to
protect the safety and health of New York State retail employ ees and to
reduce retail workplace violence. The proposed rule accord s with this
objective by clarifying and developing the language of Labor Law Section
27-e(5). Clarifying and developing this requirement will h elp employers
to better understand and fulﬁll their obligations under Labor Law Section
27-e(5), which will increase safety and reduce violence in r etail
workplaces.
Needs and Beneﬁts: The proposed rule is necessary because Labor Law
Section 27-e(5) provides a requirement with respect to cert ain New Y ork
State employers and the provision of silent response buttons to employees,
yet leaves certain key terms undeﬁned and key questions about the require-
ment unanswered. In light of this, Labor Law Section 27-e(6) expressly
authorizes the Commissioner to adopt rules and regulations necessary to
implement the provisions of this section.
By clarifying and developing the requirements at Labor Law S ection
27-e(5) as necessary for implementation, the proposed rule will beneﬁt
both employers and employees. Employers will have greater c ertainty as
to their obligations under the statute, and employees will a lso have more
certainty as to their protections under the statute. Employees will also ben-
eﬁt from being sure that any mobile phone-based or wearable s ilent re-
sponse buttons with which they are provided may not track them after an
emergency situation has ended and that employers may not ret aliate
against them for triggering a silent response button in good faith or
accidentally. Finally, employers, employees, and retail c ustomers will all
beneﬁt from having safer workplaces.
Costs: The proposed rule does not impose any additional cost s on
employers beyond what is required under Labor Law Section 27 -e(5).
Compliance costs with Labor Law Section 27-e(5) will vary based on how
employers choose to comply with the statutory requirement, how many
locations or employees they must consider, and whether they can ac-
complish compliance through current practices or need to de velop new
capacities or purchase new equipment. The proposed rule cla riﬁes the
existing statutory requirement in several ways, including by deﬁning ad-
ditional terms, specifying that multiple employees can be directed by their
employer to utilize the same silent response button in some circumstances,
and indicating that not every silent response button needs to be easily ac-
cessible to every employee so long as at least one device is ea sily acces-
sible to every employee throughout their shift. By ensuring that employers
have ﬂexibility in meeting the requirements of Labor Law Sec tion 27-
e(5), while still making sure that the section’s purpose of preventing retail
workplace violence is effectuated, the Department of Labor (department)
aims to keep the costs associated with Labor Law Section 27-e(5) reason-
able and as expected. Finally, the proposed rule can be imple mented by
the department using its existing resources.
Local Government Mandate: The proposed rule does not impose any
program, service, duty, or other responsibility upon any county, city, town,
village, school district, ﬁre district, or other special di strict. In deﬁning
covered employers, Labor Law Section 27-e explicitly excludes, “the state,
any political subdivision of the state, a public authority, or any other
governmental agency or instrumentality thereof.” The prop osed rule
adopts this deﬁnition and its exclusions.
Paperwork: The proposed rule does not impose any reporting o r
paperwork requirements.
Duplication: The proposed rule does not duplicate, overlap , or conﬂict
with any other State or Federal legal requirements. The proposed rule does
not modify or add on to the standards and requirements of Labor Law Sec-
tion 27-e(5), it only clariﬁes and develops those standards and
requirements.
Alternatives: The department considered multiple alterna tives before
deciding on the ﬁnal text of the proposed rule. For example, t he depart-
ment considered not issuing regulations related to Labor La w Section
27-e; however, not issuing regulations would have left both employers and
employees unsure of how the law would be enforced. Therefore , the
department decided to develop regulations in order to clari fy the obliga-
tions of covered employers under the law.
The department also considered alternative approaches to c ertain
aspects of the proposed rule. For example, the department co nsidered
requiring covered employers to provide each individual emp loyee with
their own silent response button or requiring employers to ensure that each
silent alarm button was accessible to all employees, but thi s would have
increased costs for employers and reduced employer ﬂexibil ity without
necessarily resulting in greater safety for beneﬁts for employees. There-
fore, the department decided against this approach and deve loped these
regulations instead, which provide greater ﬂexibility and less imposed
costs.
Federal Standards: There are not comparable Federal standards related
to preventing retail workplace violence.
NYS Register/September 30, 2026Rule Making Activities
28

Compliance Schedule: The proposed rule will be effective upon publi-
cation of a Notice of Adoption in the State Register.
Regulatory Flexibility Analysis
A Regulatory Flexibility Analysis is not required because t he Depart-
ment of Labor has determined that the proposed rule will not i mpose an
adverse economic impact on small businesses or local govern ments and
will not impose reporting, recordkeeping, or other complia nce require-
ments on small businesses or local governments.
The proposed rule does not apply to local governments. In deﬁ ning
covered employers, Labor Law Section 27-e(1)(a) explicitl y excludes,
“the state, any political subdivision of the state, a public authority, or any
other governmental agency or instrumentality thereof.” The proposed rule
adopts this deﬁnition and its exclusions.
The proposed rule also does not apply to small businesses. Ne w York
State Economic Development Law Section 131 deﬁnes a small business as
a business which, “ …employs one hundred or less persons.” While small
businesses are included in the general deﬁnition of employe r in Labor
Law Section 27-e(1)(a), which states that an employer is, “a ny person,
entity business, corporation, partnership, limited liabi lity company, or an
association employing at least ten retail employees;” Labo r Law Section
27-e(5) narrows the scope of covered employers to only large employers
for purposes of implementing silent response buttons by sta ting, “every
employer of ﬁve hundred or more retail employees statewide shall provide
every retail employee with a silent response button …” The proposed rule
only applies to employers meeting the “500 or more retail emp loyees
statewide,” threshold.
Therefore, because the proposed rule will only apply to private employ-
ers with 500 or more retail employees statewide, it will have no adverse
impact on small businesses and local governments.
Rural Area Flexibility Analysis
Types and estimated numbers of rural areas: The proposed rule does not
apply to local governments. In deﬁning covered employers, L abor Law
Section 27-e(1)(a) explicitly excludes, “the state, any political subdivision
of the state, a public authority, or any other governmental a gency or
instrumentality thereof.” The proposed rule adopts this de ﬁnition and its
exclusions.
The proposed rule does apply to certain private employers. In deﬁning
covered employers, Labor Law Section 27-e(1)(a) states that an employer
is, “any person, entity business, corporation, partnershi p, limited liability
company, or an association employing at least ten retail employees.” Ad-
ditionally, Labor Law Section 27-e(5) states that, “every employer of ﬁve
hundred or more retail employees statewide shall provide every retail em-
ployee with a silent response button …” The proposed rule adopts this def-
inition and the 500 or more retail employees statewide thres hold for the
silent response button requirement.
Therefore, the proposed rule will apply to private employers operating
across the entire State with 500 or more retail employees sta tewide, in
both rural and non-rural areas alike. With the most recently available data,
the Department of Labor (department) estimates there are over 70 covered
employers operating just over 1,000 covered locations in rural areas. Those
employers must comply with the requirements included in Lab or Law
Section 27-e(5) at their rural locations as well as their non-rural locations.
Reporting, recordkeeping and other compliance requiremen ts; and
professional services: The proposed rule does not impose any reporting or
recordkeeping requirements. As for compliance requiremen ts, the
proposed rule clariﬁes the compliance requirements in Labor Law Section
27-e(5) by specifying that multiple employees can be direct ed by their
employer to utilize the same silent response button in some circumstances
and indicating that not every silent response button needs to be easily ac-
cessible to every employee so long as at least one device is ea sily acces-
sible to every employee throughout their shift. Also, the pr oposed rule
requires employers to provide their employees with instructions on how to
use the silent response button that they have been provided. Finally, profes-
sional services are not required to comply with the proposed rule regard-
less of whether compliance is required in a rural or non-rural area.
Costs: The proposed rule does not impose any additional costs beyond
what is required under Labor Law Section 27-e(5), which requires all New
York State employers with 500 or more retail employees in New York
State to provide every retail employee with a silent respons e button to
request immediate assistance in case of an emergency. The proposed rule
clariﬁes this existing statutory requirement in several wa ys, including by
deﬁning additional terms, specifying that multiple employ ees can be
directed by their employer to utilize the same silent respon se button in
some circumstances, and indicating that not every silent re sponse button
needs to be easily accessible to every employee so long as at least one de-
vice is easily accessible to every employee throughout thei r shift. By
ensuring that employers have ﬂexibility in meeting the requ irements of
Labor Law Section 27-e(5), while still making sure that the s ection’s
purpose of preventing retail workplace violence is effectuated, the depart-
ment aims to keep the costs associated with Labor Law Section 27-e(5)
reasonable and as expected.
Minimizing adverse impact: The proposed rule will not have an adverse
economic impact on rural areas. It will protect the lives, safety, and health
of both rural and non-rural covered retail workers by clarif ying and
developing the requirements of Labor Law Section 27-e(5). In developing
the proposed rule, the department did not identify a need to establish dif-
ferent compliance or reporting requirements or timetables that would take
into account the resources available to rural areas. Simila rly, the depart-
ment did not include exemptions from coverage by the propose d rule
because doing so would endanger the public health, safety, a nd general
welfare and because such exemptions are not permissible und er Labor
Law Section 27-e. Furthermore, the proposed rule was drafted so as to al-
low ﬂexibility for all covered employers, including those i n rural areas,
while still ensuring that the statute’s purpose of preventing retail workplace
violence is effectuated.
Rural area participation: As the proposed rule was develope d, the
department consulted with representatives of the regulate d community
with statewide constituencies, including those in rural ar eas. The depart-
ment conducted listening sessions before drafting, and then provided drafts
of the regulation to impacted industry groups, including th e Business
Council of New Y ork State, the Retail, Wholesale, and Department Store
Union, the Food Industry Alliance of New York State, and the R etail
Council of New York State. Once the groups’ members reviewed and
submitted feedback, the department incorporated where pos sible and as
appropriate. Upon this publication, the department will no tify partners,
through both the distribution list of employers who signed up for updates
from the department and in direct communications, that the p ublic com-
ment period has opened and how to comment or otherwise contac t the
department. Further, the department will continue to devel op resources
and guidance for employers in rural areas that will be affect ed by the
proposed rule.
The department will continue to develop resources and guida nce for
employers in rural areas that will be affected by the propose d rule. The
proposed rule is not applicable to public employers and therefore there are
no public interests in rural areas directly affected by the proposed rule.
Job Impact Statement
A Job Impact Statement is not required because the Department of Labor
has determined that the proposed rule will not have a substan tial adverse
impact on jobs and employment opportunities. New Y ork State Labor Law
Section 27-e(5) requires all New Y ork State employers with 5 00 or more
retail employees in New Y ork State to provide every retail employee with
a silent response button to request immediate assistance in case of an
emergency. The proposed rule, promulgated pursuant to Labo r Law Sec-
tion 27-e(6), clariﬁes this existing statutory requiremen t in several ways,
including by deﬁning additional terms, specifying that multiple employees
can be directed by their employer to utilize the same silent response button
in some circumstances, and indicating that not every silent response but-
ton needs to be easily accessible to every employee so long as at least one
device is easily accessible to every employee throughout th eir shift.
Because the proposed rule primarily clariﬁes the statutory requirement
and does not impose additional obligations on employers, it will not have
a substantial adverse impact on jobs and employment opportu nities.
Instead, increasing the safety of retail workers will may ha ve a positive
impact on jobs and employment opportunities.

Purpose: To ensure adequate submetering equipment, consumer protec -

Substance of proposed rule: The Commission is considering the petition

and affordable units at 147-27 Archer Avenue, Jamaica, New Y ork located

Inc. (Con Edison). The Commission is also considering the Ow ner’s
request for a waiver of 16 NYCRR § 96.5(k)(3), which requires an energy
audit for buildings where 20 percent or more of the residents receive
income-based housing assistance.
In the petition, Archer Ave Holdings LLC requests authorization to
take electric service from Con Edison and then distribute an d meter
that electricity to its residents. Once approved by the Comm ission,
submetering of electricity to residential tenants is allowed so long as it
complies with the protections and requirements of the Commi ssion’s
regulations in 16 NYCRR Part 96.
The Owner states that, because the building is new construct ion
subject to the current New Y ork State Energy Conservation Construc-
tion Code, which provides strict energy conservation requirements for
new and renovated buildings, including the design and construction of
energy-efﬁcient building envelopes, mechanical, lightin g and power
systems, an energy audit is not necessary.

ﬂexible interconnection demonstration project.
Statutory authority: Public Service Law, sections 5, 65 and 66
Subject: A process for offering ﬂexible interconnection service options.
Purpose: To provide rules that ensure safe and adequate service at just and
reasonable rates.
Substance of proposed rule: The Public Service Commission (Commis-
sion) is considering the July 29, 2026, petition (Petition) by New York
State Electric & Gas Corporation and Rochester Gas and Electric Corpora-
tion (collectively, the Companies) seeking approval of their proposed tariff
amendments to expand the Flexible Interconnection Capacit y Solution
(FICS) demonstration project. The Companies indicate that the proposed
tariff language establishes the terms, conditions, and procedures for Flex-
ible Interconnection applications for distributed energy resources (DERs)
to interconnect at Company-identiﬁed locations. The Compa nies explain
that the proposed expansion and tariff amendments are intended to enable
increased utilization of existing distribution capacity b y DERs while
maintaining safe and reliable operation of the electric grid.
The full text of the Petition and the full record of the procee ding
may be reviewed online at the Department of Public Service web page:

ACTION PENDING
INDEX
The action pending index is a list of all proposed rules which
are currently being considered for adoption. A proposed rule is
added to the index when the notice of proposed rule making is
ﬁrst published in the
Register . A proposed rule is removed
from the index when any of the following occur: (1) the pro-
posal is adopted as a permanent rule; (2) the proposal is
rejected and withdrawn from consideration; or (3) the propos-
al’s notice expires.
Most notices expire in approximately 12 months if the agency
does not adopt or reject the proposal within that time. The
expiration date is printed in the second column of the action
pending index. Some notices, however, never expire. Those
notices are identiﬁed by the word “exempt” in the second
column. Actions pending for one year or more are preceded by
an asterisk(*).
For additional information concerning any of the proposals
listed in the action pending index, use the identiﬁcation number
to locate the text of the original notice of proposed rule making.
The identiﬁcation number contains a code which identiﬁes the
agency, the issue of the
Register in which the notice was
printed, the year in which the notice was printed and the no-
tice’s serial number. The following diagram shows how to read
identiﬁcation number codes.

code

number

Action codes: P — proposed rule making; EP — emergency
and proposed rule making (expiration date refers to proposed
rule); RP — revised rule making

Sealed bids for Project Nos. 45791-C, 45791-H, 45791-P, and
45791-E, comprising separate contracts for Construction Work, HV AC
Work, Plumbing Work, and Electrical Work, Replace Roofs & Repair
Facade, Building 73, Creedmoor Psychiatric Center, 80-45 Winchester
Blvd, Queens Village (Queens County), NY , will be received b y the
Ofﬁce of General Services (OGS), Design & Construction Grou p
(D&C), Division of Contract Management, 35th Fl., Corning T ower,
Empire State Plaza, Albany, NY 12242, on behalf of the Ofﬁce o f
Mental Health, until 2:00 p.m. on Wednesday, October 7, 2026, when
they will be publicly opened and read. Each bid must be prepared and
submitted in accordance with the Instructions to Bidders and must be
accompanied by a bid security (i.e. certiﬁed check, bank check, or bid
bond in the amount of $385,000 for C, $22,600 for H, $16,500 for P ,
and $22,600 for E).
All successful bidders will be required to furnish a Perform ance
Bond and a Labor and Material Bond pursuant to Sections 136 an d
137 of the State Finance Law, each for 100% of the amount of the
Contract estimated to be between $15,000,000 and $20,000,000 for C,
between $250,000 and $500,000 for H, between $100,000 and
$250,000 for P , and between $250,000 and $500,000 for E.
Designated staff are Megan Whaley, Catherine Skaczkowski, Jes-
sica Hoffman, and Pierre Alric in the Division of Contract Ma nage-
ment, telephone (518) 474-0203, fax (518) 473-7862.
Pursuant to Public Buildings Law § 8(6), effective January 1 1,
2020, for any projects where the project design commenced on or af-
ter January 1, 2020 and for any contracts over $5,000 for the work of
construction, reconstruction, alteration, repair, or improvement of any
State building, a responsible and reliable NYS-certiﬁed Mi nority or
Women-Owned Business Enterprise that submits a bid within t en
percent of the lowest bid will be deemed the apparent low bidd er
provided that the bid is $1,737,918 or less, adjusted annually for inﬂa-
tion as of March 1, 2026. If more than one responsible and reli able
MWBE ﬁrm meets these requirements, the MWBE ﬁrm with the low-
est bid will be deemed the apparent low bidder.

Pursuant to New York State Executive Law Article 15-A and the
rules and regulations promulgated thereunder, OGS is requi red to
promote opportunities for the maximum feasible participation of New
Y ork State-certiﬁed Minority and Women-owned Business Enterprises
(“MWBEs”) and the employment of minority group members and
women in the performance of OGS contracts. All bidders are expected
to cooperate in implementing this policy. OGS hereby establ ishes an
overall goal of 30% for MWBE participation, 15% for Minority -
Owned Business Enterprises (“MBE”) participation and 15% f or
Women-Owned Business Enterprises (“WBE”) participation ( based
on the current availability of qualiﬁed MBEs and WBEs) for th e
Construction work, and an overall goal of 10% for MWBE participa-
tion, 5% for Minority-Owned Business Enterprises (“MBE”) participa-
tion and 5% for Women-Owned Business Enterprises (“WBE”)
participation (based on the current availability of qualiﬁed MBEs and
WBEs) for the HV AC work, Plumbing work and Electrical work. The
total contract goal can be obtained by utilizing any combina tion of
MBE and /or WBE participation for subcontracting and suppli es
acquired under this Contract. Trades with 0% goals are encouraged to
make “good faith efforts” to promote and assist in the participation of
MWBEs on the Contract for the provision of services and materials.
Article 3 of the V eteran’s Services Law provides for more meaning-
ful participation in public procurement by certiﬁed Servic e-Disabled
Veteran-Owned Businesses (“SDVOBs”). Bidders are expecte d to
consider SDVOBs in the fulﬁllment of the requirements of the
Contract. Such participation may be as subcontractors or suppliers, as
protégés, or in other partnering or supporting roles. OGS he reby
establishes overall goals for SDVOBs’ participation under this
contract as follows: 6% for the C trade contractor,3% for the E trade
contractor, 3% for the H trade contractor, and 3% for the P tra de
contractor, based on the current availability of qualiﬁed S DVOBs.
Trades with 0% goals are encouraged to make “good faith efforts” to
promote and assist in the participation of SDVOBs on the Contract for
the provision of services and materials.

Memorial Justice Assistance Grant (JAG) FFY 2026 program solicita-
tion, the NYS Division of Criminal Justice Services (Division) hereby
gives notice regarding New Y ork State’s application for such funding:

tion requesting New Y ork State’s federal ﬁscal year (FFY) 2026 award
of $10,207,766, appropriated under the Edward Byrne Memori al
Justice Assistance Grant (JAG) Program. The application ge nerally
describes the proposed program activities for the four-yea r grant pe-
riod of 10/1/25 to 9/30/29 and the types of programs for which fund-

PUBLIC NOTICE
Department of Health
The New Y ork State Department of Health (Department) is required
by the provisions of the federal Beaches Environmental Asse ssment
and Coastal Health (BEACH) Act to provide for public review a nd
comment on the Department’s beach monitoring and notiﬁcation plan.
The BEACH Act (Section 406(b) of the Clean Water Act) enacted a
federal Environmental Protection Agency grant program ava ilable to
states, such as New York, with coastal recreational waters. Coastal
recreational waters include the Great Lakes and marine coastal waters
that are designated for swimming, bathing, surﬁng, or simil ar water
contact activities. The Act is not applicable to inland waters or waters
upstream of the mouth of a river or stream having an unimpaired natu-
ral connection with the open sea.
The beach monitoring and public notiﬁcation plan also includes in-
formation on the beach evaluation and classiﬁcation proces s, includ-
ing a list of waters to be monitored and beach ranking. Also included
in this plan, is the sampling design and monitoring plan, inc luding
sampling location and sampling frequency. Lastly, the plan contains
information on procedures for public notiﬁcation and risk communica-
tion, including methods to notify the public of a swimming ad visory
or beach closure.
Any interested parties and/or agencies desiring to review a nd/or
comment on the beach monitoring and notiﬁcation plan for coa stal
recreational waters may do so by writing to : Eric J. Wiegert, MPH,
Director, Bureau of Community Environmental Health and Foo d
Protection, Center for Environmental Health, Department o f Health,
Empire State Plaza, Corning Tower Bldg., Rm. 1395, Albany, N Y
12237

Department of State
F-2026-0408
Date of Issuance – September 30, 2026
The New York State Department of State (DOS) is required by
Federal regulations to provide timely public notice for the activities
described below, which are subject to the consistency provi sions of
the Federal Coastal Zone Management Act of 1972, as amended.
The applicant has certiﬁed that the proposed activity complies with
and will be conducted in a manner consistent with the approved New
Y ork State Coastal Management Program.
In F-2026-0408, NYS Department of Environmental Conservat ion
is proposing to construct a new ﬁsh spawning reef measuring 15 feet
wide x 35 feet long x 4 feet deep placed in 12-15 feet of water.
Off the south shore of Dablon Point, Mud Bay, Lake Ontario, Town
of Cape Vincent, Jefferson County.
The stated purpose of the proposed action is to support nativ e ﬁsh
conservation efforts for Walleye, Lake Whiteﬁsh and Cisco.
The applicant’s consistency certiﬁcation and supporting i nforma-
tion are available for review at: https://dos.ny.gov/f-20 26-0408 or at
https://dos.ny.gov/public-notices
Original copies of public information and data submitted by the ap-
plicant are available for inspection at the New Y ork State Department
of State ofﬁces located at One Commerce Plaza, 99 Washington Ave-
nue, in Albany, New Y ork.
Any interested parties and/or agencies desiring to express their
views concerning any of the above proposed activities may do so by

the date of publication of this notice, or October 15, 2026.

90

EXECUTIVEORDERS
Executive Order No. 47.22: Declaring a Disaster Emergency i n
the State of New Y ork and Ordering into Active Service the New
Y ork National Guard to Assist Authorities in Guaranteeing Public
Order and Protection of Public Property at Various Correctional
Facilities
WHEREAS, on February 17, 2025, and continuing thereafter, an il-
legal and unlawful strike by correction ofﬁcers has created an im-
minent threat to the safety of correction ofﬁcers who are currently on
the job, the more than 32,290 individuals in the Department o f Cor-
rections and Community Supervision’s care and the communities sur-
rounding these correctional facilities;
WHEREAS, upon review, there exists a continued need to support
staff, provide for the safety of those under the care of the Department
of Corrections and Community Supervision, preserve staff resources,
and ensure safe operations at correctional facilities statewide;
NOW THEREFORE, I, Kathy Hochul, Governor of the State of
New Y ork, by virtue of the authority vested in me by the Constitution
of the State of New Y ork and Section 28 of Article 2-B of the Execu-
tive Law, do hereby ﬁnd a continued need for a statewide disas ter
emergency order to support safe and effective operations at cor-
rectional facilities. This executive order shall be in effe ct through
October 11, 2026.
FURTHER, by virtue of the authority vested in me by Section 29-a
of Article 2-B of the Executive Law do hereby temporarily suspend or
modify the following laws, all of which have been suspended s ince
February 19, 2025, through October 11, 2026:
D Section 97-G of the State Finance Law, to the extent necessary
to purchase food, supplies, services, and equipment or furn ish or
provide various centralized services to assist affected lo cal govern-
ments, individuals, and other non-State entities in respon ding to and
recovering from the emergency;
D Section 112 of the State Finance Law, to the extent consisten t
with Article V , Section I of the State Constitution, and to th e extent
necessary to add additional work, sites and time to State contracts; and
D Section 163 of the State Finance Law, to the extent necessary to
purchase commodities, services, technology and materials without
following the standard notice and procurement processes.
(L.S.) GIVEN under my hand and the Privy Seal of
the State in the City of Albany this 11th day
of September in the year two thousand
twenty-six.
BY THE GOVERNOR
/S/ Kathy Hochul
/s/ Karen Persichilli Keogh
Secretary to the Governor
